**P19-B6.5a shipped 2026-06-17 — the system can now turn crypto trading on by itself, without also waking the unfinished tokenized-stock side ✅** (run autonomously with Langston per Kyle's directive · `P19_B6_5a_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5/§6). This is the first half of the crypto-resurrection work, and it exists because of what the planning step found: the system had only ONE on/off switch for active trading per mode, and flipping it on would have woken up both crypto AND the still-incomplete xStock side at once — there was no way to turn on just crypto. That's a real problem, because the planned real turn-on (B7b) goes crypto-first with xStock deliberately held off, so it needs exactly this per-asset switch. Langston ruled (overriding my first instinct to use a throwaway test rig) that the right move was to build the switch properly now, since it's required infrastructure the turn-on needs regardless. So this batch added a small, default-OFF on/off flag for each asset class, layered as an extra safety gate on top of the existing master switch: a class trades only if the master is on AND that class's own flag is on. A missing flag counts as off, so it fails safe. Both crypto and xStock ship off, so nothing about today's behavior changes — the gate can only ever restrict. The proof that matters is a test showing crypto can be turned on while xStock stays completely silent (zero activity), which is the precondition the eventual turn-on depends on. The switch-flipping is built the same careful way as the master switch (save to the database first, then announce), and flipping one class never disturbs another. Langston's code review approved it with two small notes, both handled: a redundant line was removed, and one not-yet-used safety hook was given a scheduled home in the next batch (where it'll either be wired up or removed once the audit proves it's unnecessary). Bench green (typecheck no-regression + 47 targeted tests including the isolation test), CI all-four-green, deployed and independently verified on the live server — the new flag reads empty (off) on both modes, the engine is idle, and the system boots clean. Next: B6.5b — the crypto-only test run through this new switch, plus the audit of the whole crypto chain.

**B-GOV shipped 2026-06-17 — a self-checking watchdog for our own process: it catches when a batch's governance paperwork doesn't get done, names exactly what's missing, and (once switched on) keeps nagging until it's actually fixed. Built and proven, deliberately left OFF until a follow-up makes it safe to run live ✅** (standalone governance batch; run autonomously with Langston; Kyle drove the design · `BATCH_B_GOV_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). The problem this addresses: our written governance is good but enforcement leaks — documents that should update at a batch's close sometimes don't, and we only catch it when Kyle happens to ask (a real recent case: one batch shipped with no pre-audit on file). Kyle's bar was "rock-solid or I keep the small leaks," so the design was deliberately conservative. After several rounds with Langston (and rejecting side-branches, a Discord agent, and a sprawling 18-piece version), it converged on a clean split: a **mechanical bot** for the facts a machine can't get wrong — is each required document there, is it real content or just a date-bump, was the pre-audit filed and does it cite the right reference maps, and did the paperwork follow the code within four hours — and **Langston for the judgment calls** a machine can't make (is a document actually thorough, is a skip legitimate). Anything the bot flags rides our existing every-turn alert list and stays lit until the gap is genuinely closed. The honest limit, stated plainly throughout: it detects and drives the fix, it does **not** physically block anything (blocking would need a side branch Kyle ruled out). It was proven against our real history — it correctly catches that one batch's missing pre-audit and does not false-alarm on properly-closed batches — and the four-hour reminder window was validated against data (our last 13 closes all finished within four hours; most within forty minutes). Langston's code review caught two real bugs before they shipped (an alert that would never clear itself once the document was added, and a name-matching flaw that confused two similarly-named batches), both fixed with new tests. It ships **switched off** — it only runs on demand today — and a clearly-scoped follow-up (B-GOV-2) must land before it can run as a live, always-on watcher. Kyle's new requirement that every batch and phase be named consistently was written into the rules so the watchdog can read those names unambiguously. Bench green (typecheck no-regression + 23 checker tests + the history-replay gate), CI all-four-green. Next: per the Phase-19 sequence (B-GOV is orthogonal to the paper-trading turn-on work).

**P19-B6.5b shipped 2026-06-17 — five fixes that finish the per-asset switch and patch real holes in the crypto trading path, then a controlled test-run revealed exactly where crypto signals were dying ✅** (run autonomously with Langston per Kyle's directive · `P19_B6_5b_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5/§6). This is the second half of the crypto-resurrection work. It closes five gaps the first half left open. One: the new per-asset switch only guarded the doorway where signals join the buy queue, but the scanner that fills the candidate pools was still going by the old master switch — so the switch is now honored at the scanner too, plus a backstop rejection at the queue door, plus a sweep that clears already-in-flight candidates if a class is switched off mid-cycle. Two: a safety witness that proves the dormant side stays silent was built earlier but never actually called — now wired in. Three: the "wait a bit before re-trading the same coin" cooldown was reading the wrong records in paper mode (an old empty table), so it silently never applied — now pointed at the real paper-trade records. Four: a never-closing-position hole where, if a volatility reading came back as zero, the hard stop-loss and take-profit checks were skipped entirely — now they always run. A dead, unused leftover piece of buy-queue code was also deleted. The important part: a **crypto-only trial run** was done on the staging server and then switched back off — and it proved the whole front half of the pipeline works (the scanner, the pools, the decision engine, and the quality check all fired for crypto with no crash), while pinpointing that signals were being rejected at the final "add to buy queue" step by two database problems. Those two are handed to the next batch (B6.5c). Langston reviewed and approved the code, confirming the two trickiest safety details were correct. Next: B6.5c — fix the two buy-queue rejections the trial run found.

**P19-B6.5c shipped 2026-06-17 — the two database problems blocking every crypto signal from the buy queue are fixed, and crypto signals now reach the buy queue under their proper strategy names; one new downstream blocker remains before a full trade can complete ⏳** (run autonomously with Langston per Kyle's directive · `P19_B6_5c_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5/§6). The previous trial run showed crypto signals were being thrown out at the final "add to buy queue" step by two separate problems, each of which was rejecting 100% of crypto signals. The first: the live database still had an old leftover required column that the code had already stopped filling in, so every single insert failed — fixed by properly dropping that column with a tested, reversible database change so the fix applies on any server, not just one. The second was a genuine concept error worth stating plainly, and Kyle corrected it directly: the pattern detector was inventing made-up strategy names (like "pattern_pinbar") that aren't real strategies at all. **Patterns are not strategies — they are triggers that feed the system's nineteen fixed, official strategies; we never invent new strategies.** So each detected pattern is now matched to the actual official strategy that consumes it, correctly accounting for the current market regime and asset class, and if there's no clean match the signal is honestly dropped rather than forced onto the nearest strategy (which would have quietly corrupted that strategy's performance statistics). A redundant duplicate piece of signal-emitting code was removed as part of this. **What's proven:** in another controlled crypto trial run, signals now reach the buy queue carrying their real strategy names (e.g. "reverse_impulse," "inside_bar_reversal"), with zero made-up names and zero database errors, the tokenized-stock side stayed fully isolated, and the "no clean match, dropped" counter behaved sensibly per market regime. **What's NOT yet done:** the goal of watching at least one complete crypto trade run start-to-finish (open, exit, close, cooldown) is NOT met — a new, separate downstream break surfaced in this run, where signals that pass the buy queue and get sized still fail to actually OPEN a position (the hand-off into the trade-execution engine silently fails). Per Langston's ruling that proof-and-repair shouldn't balloon, that open-path repair is carried to its own new batch, **P19-B6.5e**, which now owns that full-trade-lifecycle proof. CI all-four-green; deployed and live-verified on staging. Langston reviewed and approved the full change. Next: B6.5e — repair the open-path so a crypto trade can complete end to end.

**P19-B6.5d shipped 2026-06-18 — a trading pair now carries its own "what kind of asset am I" label all the way through the pipeline instead of having it re-guessed from the symbol at every step; this fixed a live alert where a real coin was being thrown out ✅** (run autonomously with Langston per Kyle's directive · `P19_B6_5d_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). This batch started from a live red alert: the pair `A/EUR` (the coin "Vaulta," which trades on Kraken) was being rejected on the active path because the system couldn't decide what asset class it was. Digging in revealed a structural problem, not a one-off: at 26 of the 35 places the system needs to know an asset's class, it was RE-GUESSING that class from the symbol text every time, rather than reading a label set once when the pair entered the pipeline. Re-guessing is fragile — it silently assumes the exchange is Kraken and mishandles the handful of tickers that exist as BOTH a tokenized stock and a crypto coin, plus any single-letter coin name. Per the no-patches rule, the whole pattern was fixed rather than just the one alert. The principle now enforced: a pair is STAMPED with its asset class once, at the moment it enters the pipeline, and that stamp is CARRIED with it through every downstream step (quality check, buy queue, exit logic, execution) — never re-derived from the symbol text again. The specific alert was cleared by allowing single-letter coin names to be recognized (done through one shared setting so the rule can't drift apart in two places), while carefully preserving the precedence that keeps a single-letter coin from ever masquerading as one of the tokenized-stock collision tickers. Verified directly against the running server: A/EUR now correctly reads as crypto, the genuine tokenized-stock tickers still read as tokenized stock, and the collision tickers still get their drift warning. The repeated alert was also de-duplicated so it fires once per pair instead of spamming. Fourteen places that fill in defaults were made fail-safe on the active path and clearly logged on the learning-only path. Fourteen new tests were added. The learning/simulation side was deliberately left as-is by design — it resolves the class once at entry for telemetry and is correct that way; this is written down so a future code search doesn't mistake it for a missed spot. Pure code change, no database migration. Bench green (typecheck no-regression + 14 new tests), CI all-four-green, deployed and confirmed on staging with both trading engines off. Langston reviewed, approved, and independently re-ran the resolver and the full test suite on the deployed code to confirm. Next: per the Phase-19 sequence (B6.5e — the crypto open-path repair).

**P19-B6.5e shipped 2026-06-18 — the spot where a ready-to-buy crypto signal was silently vanishing instead of opening a trade is now fully visible and accounted for, and that visibility revealed the real reason no crypto trade opens: the system is correctly turning every crypto signal down because its profit-vs-cost math is running on placeholder inputs ✅** (run autonomously with Langston per Kyle's directive · `P19_B6_5e_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). The previous batch left one blocker: a sized crypto signal would clear the buy queue and then never actually open a position, with no error and no reason — the system's own counter showed signals were "attempted" but neither opened nor blocked, so they were disappearing into a blind spot. The root cause was exactly that — a blind spot: the open step didn't report a result, it just ran and quietly stopped at any of about seven exit points without recording why, and the layer above it guessed "did it work?" by checking whether the trade count went up. So a failed open looked identical to nothing happening. The fix made the open step report a real, typed outcome and added a third counting category — "open failed, and here's the stage it failed at" — so the system's open-path tally now always balances (attempts equals opened plus blocked plus open-failed) and can never hide a vanished signal again. This was proven on the live server with a contained crypto trial run that was switched back off afterward: the counter reconciled and named the exact stage every time. **And naming the stage is what produced the real diagnosis:** the crypto open is NOT broken in execution — it runs all the way to the profit-expectancy gate and is HONESTLY turned down there, because after trading costs the expected value comes out slightly negative on every crypto signal. The gate is working correctly and must not be weakened. The actual reason it never clears is that the profit math is being fed placeholder inputs — the same default win-probability and market-condition numbers on every signal instead of each signal's real values — which is a previously-known gap. Feeding the real inputs (not touching the open path) is the lever that will let genuine signals clear and open. That work, plus the goal of finally watching one complete crypto trade run start-to-finish, is carried to a new batch, **P19-B6.5g**; the real turn-on stays gated on it. The trial run also surfaced six pairs being thrown out because a handful of newer Kraken price-currencies aren't recognized yet, so a small batch to complete that currency list, **P19-B6.5f**, was created and goes first. A dead leftover import was also removed. Bench green (typecheck clean + 6 new tests), CI all-four-green, deployed and confirmed on staging with both engines off afterward. Langston reviewed and approved. Next: B6.5f (the currency-list completion), then B6.5g (the profit-input fix plus the full crypto-trade proof).

**B-XSTOCK-GLOBALS shipped 2026-06-18 — the tokenized-stock learning trades were showing blank in their three "whole-market" columns, and that's now fixed and verified on screen ✅** (Claude Old implemented; Kyle reassigned it from Claude New · `B_XSTOCK_GLOBALS_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). On the machine-learning page, every tokenized-stock trade was leaving its three market-wide readings — the overall regime, the overall friction, and the overall directional bias — blank ("—" and "pending"), even though the system was computing those readings perfectly well every cycle. The cause was a handoff gap: an earlier batch changed the shared trade-recording step so it no longer fills those values in by itself — each side now has to hand them in when a trade opens. The crypto side was updated to do that; the tokenized-stock side never was, so its trades recorded blanks. The fix has the tokenized-stock trade-open step read those three live values (which were already sitting computed in memory) and attach them, exactly mirroring what crypto does. Verified on the staging screen and in the data: the values now populate; trades that opened in the same minute show identical readings (proving it's one shared whole-market snapshot, not something miscalculated per trade); and the values correctly drift over time with the live market (the friction reading in particular is the measured average spread across the tokenized-stock market, which is very wide and "frozen" right now — a real market signal, not an error). A loud tripwire to catch this exact problem if it ever recurs was split off into its own small follow-up. Bench green, CI all-four-green, deployed and confirmed on staging. Langston reviewed and approved. Next: per the Phase-19 sequence.

**B-GOV-2 shipped 2026-06-19 — the governance watchdog from B-GOV is now built to run for real and installed on the server, but deliberately left paused after its first run flooded our alert list, pending a short calibration before it pages anyone ⏳** (Claude Old; Langston-reviewed · `BATCH_B_GOV_2_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). B-GOV built the watchdog but left it switched off; B-GOV-2 is the work that makes it safe to run live. It adds the pieces the watchdog needed to be trustworthy: each batch now declares its "size" (how much paperwork it owes) in its plan document; a guard that flags when a batch looks bigger than it declared; a separate "is the watchdog itself still alive?" heartbeat; correct handling of long autonomous batches so the deadline clock doesn't false-alarm; and a "watch-only" calibration mode for the first little while. It's installed on the staging server as its own isolated job. **Honest account of a misstep:** I switched the timer on before first teaching it which of our older batches are already finished — so its very first run flagged every historical batch (88 of them) at once, and because those flags posted as low-priority items and our alert-reading rule shows all active items regardless of priority, they briefly flooded the shared alert list we all read. I caught it on my own next check, switched the watchdog back off, and cleared all 88 — the list is clean and nothing's running. It actually revealed a real design gap worth fixing now rather than after go-live: "watch-only mode" was supposed to keep things out of the alert list, but our alert-reading rule doesn't honor that, so watch-only didn't actually stay quiet. So two things must happen before it goes live: teach it which batches are already closed, and make watch-only genuinely silent. The encouraging part underneath: the watchdog genuinely works — on that one run it correctly flagged a real gap (the very tokenized-stock governance write-up that was overdue) and even flagged its own missing declaration. Code is in, all checks green, and the to-do list to switch it on cleanly is written down. Next: seed the "already-closed" list, fix watch-only's quiet, verify a clean run, then let it page.

**reorg-B2.1 shipped 2026-06-21 — the checks that decide whether a trade's target is worth taking now live inside the strategies themselves instead of a separate after-the-fact step, a needless target-rewrite was removed, and one inconsistent rule was unified ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston · `P19_REORG_B2_1_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). Kyle asked a sharp question: why was a separate step quietly adjusting and re-checking each trade's profit target *after* the strategy already produced it — shouldn't the strategy set its target right in the first place? Looking across all nineteen strategies confirmed he was right. Three changes followed. First, a step that used to *raise* a target up to a minimum size was removed entirely — rewriting the strategy's own target was both redundant with the main profit-vs-cost gate that already judges every trade, and dishonest about what the strategy actually wanted. Second, the two real checks — "is the reward worth the risk?" and "can the price physically reach this target?" — moved into the shared safety check that every strategy already runs at the moment it makes a signal, so the judgment lives where the signal is born. Third, a genuine inconsistency was fixed: some strategies were being held to a looser reward rule and others to a stricter one, from two different places; now there's a single per-asset-class rule, and every rejection is visibly recorded by reason (no hidden gates — Kyle's firm requirement). A new always-on tally counts, per strategy, how often each is turned down and why — this is the raw material for a later, evidence-based decision about where each strategy's reward bar should sit (that calibration is a Phase-25 item, not a guess made now). **Honest account of a misstep:** when the strategies were wired into the shared check, two automated tests broke because they didn't set up one piece of the new plumbing — and I briefly recorded the batch as passing its automated checks when it was not. I caught it while assembling the closing evidence, fixed the two tests (a test-only fix; the live system was always fine, since it loads that piece at startup), and confirmed all four automated checks pass. Both of us re-grounded on always confirming the checks are green before any push. The separate after-the-fact step is kept for now as a harmless duplicate and will be removed in the follow-up **reorg-B2.2**, which also adds the on-screen visibility of these reward-vs-risk rejections and makes the tally survive a server restart — that follow-up is being built now but, per Kyle's call, won't be deployed until a 48-hour observation window on the current changes finishes. The live code is deployed and recording; Langston reviewed and approved all of it. Next: reorg-B2.2 (built during the observation window, deployed after).

**reorg-B2.2 shipped 2026-06-23 — the per-strategy "how often was this turned down, and why" tally now splits crypto from tokenized stocks, shows up on screen in both filter-diagnostics tabs, and survives a server restart ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston · `P19_REORG_B2_2_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). This finished the two pieces held back during the 48-hour observation window. First, the tally that counts how often each strategy's trades are rejected (and for which reason — reward-too-small, target-unreachable, stop-too-far, bad volatility reading) now records which asset class each one came from, so the crypto tab and the tokenized-stock ("xStock") tab each show only their own numbers instead of the two being mixed into one confusing total — and the combined per-strategy view still reads exactly as before. Second, those rejection reasons are now visible right in the on-screen filter-diagnostics for both asset classes (Kyle's firm "no hidden gates" requirement), with an honest "no evaluations yet" state when a strategy hasn't run on that class instead of a misleading zero. Third, the tally now saves itself to disk every minute and reloads on startup, so a restart pauses-and-resumes the measurement window rather than wiping it — and it refuses to load a save-file from an older format so stale numbers can never silently corrupt the counts. The screens were checked live: the crypto tab shows one strategy active so far, the tokenized-stock tab shows three (one of them turned down on 100% of its 40 evaluations because its natural reward target sits below the current bar) — exactly the evidence we'll use to set each strategy's reward bar properly in the next step. Deployed, all four automated checks green, Langston reviewed and independently verified on the server. The one remaining piece of this batch — retiring the now-redundant old after-the-fact step — stays deliberately on hold until a separate measurement (how often the old step and the new in-strategy check disagree) is read on this now-durable per-class window. Next: reorg-B2.3 (set each strategy's reward bar per asset class from its own data).

**reorg-B3 shipped 2026-06-24 — the two real numbers the system uses to judge whether a trade is worth taking now actually reach that decision, instead of being quietly dropped and replaced with stand-in defaults ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston · `P19_REORG_B3_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). When the system decides whether to open a trade, it weighs two readings about the pair: how cleanly it's trending, and how strong its directional bias is. Both were being calculated early, when the pair is first picked, but then silently lost before the open-the-trade decision — which fell back on generic placeholder values. reorg-B3 carries both numbers, captured at the exact moment the pair was selected, all the way through to that decision, stored as their own proper database fields so they can't go missing again. Along the way I caught a genuine hidden bug: a tag that marks a signal as a "strong-trend" trade was also being dropped in a hand-off step, which meant strong-trend trades were never actually getting the better treatment they were supposed to — so the main fix would have done nothing for them. That's now fixed too, and the same one-line repair fixes several other places that quietly depended on that tag. Two honest notes. First, this is NOT a change that suddenly lets the system trade crypto — the math shows the trend-cleanliness reading was already pinned at its ceiling, so threading the real value changes nothing for it; only the directional-bias half actually moves the trade-worth math, and only for strong-trend trades. Second, all of this lives on the trading path that's currently switched off (the system is still in learning mode), so it can't be watched working live until paper-mode trading is turned back on later in this phase — for now it's proven by an automated end-to-end test (8 of 8), and I added an on-screen readout that will show these numbers reaching the decision once trading is on. One smaller gap — the tokenized stocks ("xStocks") don't yet have a source for their bias number, so they fall back to the cautious default — is written down as a tracked fix for the xStock turn-on work, not left to be forgotten. Deployed, all four automated checks green, the two new database columns confirmed live, Langston reviewed and approved the code. Next: reorg-B2.3 (set each strategy's reward bar per asset class from its own data).

**reorg-B3.1 + reorg-B3.2 shipped 2026-06-24 — the learning engine was being strangled by our own recent change, and we un-strangled it ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). Two tiny clean-ups plus the important one. **The important one (B3.2):** Kyle noticed the learning system (VTS) had nearly stopped trading — down from ~150 new simulated trades a day to 3–5. We pulled the numbers and confirmed it: a 95–97% collapse that began *exactly* when our recent reward-vs-risk gate deployed. That gate, added for the real trading path, was *also* wired into the learning engine, where it was hard-dropping every signal that didn't clear the bar. That did two harms: it starved the learning system, and it made the upcoming reward-bar calibration circular — you can't honestly set a bar from data the bar already filtered out. The fix, agreed with Langston and answering Kyle's "are we polluting the data?" concern precisely: on the learning path, the *quality* checks (reward-too-small, target-too-far) now **label** the trade and let it simulate to a close anyway — capturing the real outcome — while genuine garbage (broken volatility readings, malformed geometry) is still thrown out. The real trading path keeps the gate fully enforcing. So we get *more* learning data, not bad data — and the rejected trades' real wins/losses become the evidence that tells us where each reward bar actually belongs. Langston's code review caught a real type-safety bug that the automated check had masked (existing errors in that file hid it); fixed before deploy. **The two clean-ups (B3.1):** the trailing-exit engine now sees the real directional-integrity number at trade-open instead of a constant placeholder (#378); and the diagnostics endpoint now returns the full metrics object instead of a hand-picked subset that had been silently dropping fields (#379). Deployed (staging `c077faa7f`, restart#412), all four automated checks green, the VTS now simulating quality-gated trades live. Next: reorg-B2.3 (set each strategy's reward bar from this now-richer, un-strangled data), then reorg-B4.

**reorg-B3.3 shipped 2026-06-24 — the un-strangle, done at the place it actually had to happen ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). When we deployed the learning-engine fix above (B3.2) and watched it, the learning system stayed nearly dead — the fix wasn't working. The reason: the reward-vs-risk check actually runs in two places, one after the other, and each of our 19 trading strategies throws the signal away at the *first* place — right when it builds the trade — before it ever reaches the second place where B3.2's fix lived. So B3.2 was correct but unreachable; the real numbers confirmed it (one simulated trade across 136,779 strategy evaluations). reorg-B3.3 moves the same "label-don't-throw-away" rule to that first place, inside the strategies themselves. We added one shared decision helper and a single on/off switch that every strategy now reads: the real-trading and live paths leave it on its default (throw away on any fail — so nothing about real trading changed, by construction), and only the crypto learning path flips it to "label and keep." A reward-too-small or target-too-far signal now flows through, gets labelled, and simulates to a real close so we capture what it *would* have done; genuinely broken signals (bad volatility reading, degenerate stop) are still thrown out everywhere. Langston reviewed the whole change and signed off on all six things he wanted proven (chief among them: that "target-too-far" can only mean a valid-but-distant target, never a malformed one; and that the real-trading path is provably untouched). The xStock side of the learning engine has a differently-built gate, so it gets the same treatment in the very next batch (reorg-B3.3x) — its home is already written down so it can't be forgotten. Deployed (staging `ed9b8b626`, restart#413), all four automated checks green; the crypto learning engine is now simulating the previously-discarded trades. Next: reorg-B3.3x (the xStock half), then reorg-B4.

**reorg-B3.3y shipped 2026-06-24 — a tiny clean-up the un-strangle exposed ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). Right after the un-strangle went live, watching it surfaced a small flaw: one strategy was occasionally producing a setup whose profit target sat *at or below* the entry price — meaning the trade could never make money. Because of a one-sided check, those broken setups were being treated as merely "low quality" and kept for learning, rather than being recognized as broken and discarded. They started showing up about once a minute. The system already had a rule that throws out a trade whose stop-loss is on the wrong side of entry; it just never had the mirror-image rule for the profit target. We added that mirror — now a valid trade has to have its stop below entry *and* its target above entry, and anything that fails either side is discarded everywhere, including the learning engine. Nothing about real trading changed (those broken setups were already being thrown out there, just for a different stated reason). This keeps the learning data clean — the "low quality" bucket now only holds real, tradeable setups that merely had a thin reward, which is exactly the data we need to set each strategy's reward bar later. Langston confirmed the boundary is right and signed off; deployed (staging `2136c74fe`, restart#414), all four automated checks green. Next: reorg-B3.3x (the xStock half of the un-strangle), then reorg-B4.

**reorg-B3.3x shipped 2026-06-24 — the xStock half of the un-strangle, done by reusing the shared piece ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). This applies the same un-strangle to the xStock learning path that we did for crypto. The wrinkle: the xStock path was built separately a while back and never got wired to the shared "gate" component — the one piece that both labels each trade's verdict and (since the last batch) discards broken-geometry setups. So instead of writing a second copy of that logic just for xStock — which is exactly the kind of duplication we keep working to eliminate — we bolted the *existing shared gate* onto the xStock path. Langston was firm that this was the right call (and caught a real bug in my first pass: the verdict label I was attaching wouldn't actually have been recorded, because the shared trade-recording component had no slot for it — I added the slot and wired it through). The result: xStock now runs the identical gate crypto does, it automatically inherited the reward-leg fix from the last batch, and both asset classes are now governed by *one* shared piece instead of two. Confirmed live on the system: real xStock setups (Kroger, Moderna, Marvell among them) that were being thrown away for being just under the reward bar are now flowing through and being captured for learning, while real trading stays untouched. One honest note logged for later: these verdict labels live in active memory only, not the long-term database, for both asset classes — fine for live monitoring, but if we ever want historical lookups that's a small separate task (logged and assigned to a future batch). Deployed (staging `53f601b93`, restart#415), all four checks green; Langston independently verified the wiring end-to-end. With this, the un-strangle is complete across both crypto and xStock. Next: reorg-B4 (the shadow-trade layer), then the B6 quick-hitters.

**B-DIAG-387 shipped 2026-06-25 — the xStock dashboard tile that read zero forever (and fooled us last week) now tells the truth ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). One of the xStock diagnostics tiles is supposed to show how often a trade idea gets thrown out for not clearing the fee hurdle. It had been stuck on zero — even though those rejections were really happening and were being saved correctly in our permanent record the whole time. That stuck-on-zero tile is exactly what misled me last week into thinking xStock was clearing the fees when it wasn't (a conclusion we caught and retracted). The cause was plain once traced: the page was reading the count from an empty leftover slot in the code instead of the real running tally. The fix points it at the real tally — the same way the crypto side already worked. While in there I also surfaced three trade-block reasons that were previously invisible on the panel (a "no-hidden-gates" cleanup Kyle insists on), and I ripped out the dead leftover code that caused the zero-bug in the first place so it can't mislead us again. I proved the fix the strict way Langston asked for: I read the live number straight off the dashboard's feed and compared it, to the exact unit, against the permanent record over the same window — both said 25, a perfect match with no fudge factor. Nothing about how trades are picked, sized, or gated changed — this was display-and-truth only. This mattered enough to do *before* the next reorg piece because the upcoming fee-switching work (reorg-B7) needs a readable, trustworthy baseline on these dashboards to measure against. Deployed (staging `1c451f5b5`, restart#416), all four automated checks green, Langston reviewed and approved the plan and the code. Next: reorg-B4 (the shadow-trade layer), then the B6 quick-hitters.

**reorg-B4 shipped 2026-06-25 — the shadow-trade layer that will tell us whether we're picking the right trade ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). Our whole edge is *selection* — each cycle the system has a pool of ready-to-buy ideas and promotes the best one or two, but we've never actually measured whether the one it promoted beat the ones it passed over. This batch builds the instrument to answer that. Every time the system makes a promotion decision, it now also opens a "pretend" trade for *every* candidate in the pool — not just the one it picked — runs each through the exact same exit logic the real trades use, and records both the decision-time scores and how each pretend trade actually turned out. That's the raw material for the upcoming ranking fix (reorg-B5): with it we can finally check, on real outcomes, whether our ranking is choosing the genuine best. The hard part wasn't building it — it was guaranteeing it can *never* contaminate real trading or the learning data, since it generates a flood of extra pretend trades. We walled it off four ways: the pretend trades live in their own separate memory, their close path is only allowed to write to their own private records table, every place that reads our shared trade table for learning was given a filter to skip them, and a restart reloads them into the right (separate) bucket. Langston's code review earned its keep: he caught that I'd missed one of those four walls — three places that read the shared table for learning weren't yet skipping the pretend trades — which, left unfixed, would have quietly polluted our most important learning feed once paper trading turns back on. I fixed all three with a single reusable filter and added two tests that *prove* a pretend trade does not show up in that feed. Honest caveat, by design: this produces no data *yet*, because paper trading is still off, so nothing is being promoted — it's wired, proven correct, and sits dormant until we flip paper trading on (around the B9 step), exactly as planned. Two small hardening items are scheduled for that turn-on moment. Deployed (staging `f3cbe2d14`, restart#418), the new records table is live, all four automated checks green, and Langston independently verified everything on the live server. Next: the B6 quick-hitters (B6.6/B6.7/B6.8).

**reorg-B4.1 shipped 2026-06-26 — the Shadows tab: now you can actually see whether we're picking the best trade ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). The shadow-trade engine from the last batch had no screen — its results lived only in a database table. This batch adds a "Shadows" tab on the trading page (right after Trade History) that shows, for each promotion cycle, the full pool of ready-to-buy candidates ranked by score, a clear marker on the one we actually promoted, and how every candidate's pretend trade turned out — so at a glance you can see "we promoted the #1-ranked one, but the #2 candidate actually finished better." It also shows the pretend trades currently in flight and a running "how often did the promoted pick actually win?" summary. While building it we also closed a subtlety Kyle's questions exposed: the engine had only been recording each candidate's rank the first time it appeared, so if a candidate lingered across cycles or got promoted later, the record was stale — now every cycle records each candidate's rank and promoted-status freshly, which is exactly what makes the by-cycle "did we pick the best" view trustworthy. Langston's review caught a real honesty bug in the summary math (it was crediting the ranker before a cycle's field had fully resolved, and the headline number silently shifted as you paged) — both fixed by computing the summary in one clean query over only the fully-resolved cycles. I verified it live on the staging site exactly as Kyle asked: opened the tab, confirmed the empty state reads cleanly, then briefly dropped in a couple of sample cycles to screenshot the populated layout (it correctly showed a cycle where we promoted the #1 candidate but the #2 finished best), then removed the samples and confirmed it returned to empty. Honest note, by design: the tab stays empty until paper trading is turned back on (nothing is being promoted yet), and a small data-retention cleanup for the new per-cycle records is scheduled for that turn-on moment. Deployed (staging `153cc4235`, restart#419), all four automated checks green, Langston independently verified everything on the live server. Next: the B6 quick-hitters.

**P19-B6.6 shipped 2026-06-26 — a safety check that won't let the system fill an xStock order into a frozen market ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston). Before the system would ever fill an xStock order, it now requires that token's price to have actually moved within the last 45 minutes — proof the market is genuinely trading, not sitting frozen on a holiday, a trading halt, or a dead feed. We locked the 45-minute threshold from three days of real archived price data: it lets every actively-traded token through with room to spare while correctly declining the handful of slow ETF-style tokens whose prices only update every ~50 minutes (filling those would mean trading off a stale price). It fails safe — if a setting is missing, a database read is slow, or the feed dies, it blocks rather than fills, and it records WHY it blocked so a feed outage can never be mistaken for a holiday. Like the recent shadow-trade work, it stays dormant until paper trading is switched on, so nothing in current behavior changes; this makes the safety check exist and be correct for that switch-on. Closes the holiday/half-day hole (#236) that the older clock-based check couldn't cover.

**P19-B6.7 shipped 2026-06-26 — removed a dead second price feed that had never worked, and rebuilt the feed-health alarm that was watching it so it watches the real feed instead ✅** (run autonomously with Claude New + Langston · `P19_B6_7_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md` §1/§5). The system carried a second price-feed connection, separate from the real one that drives trading, that had delivered exactly zero prices since early April — yet because it kept a network connection open, every health and safety monitor that looked at it believed it was perfectly healthy. It was also producing a "feed is stale" log line every 30 seconds. The real danger was a Phase-19 landmine: the feed-health alarm graded that dead feed and would have fired CRITICAL "the feed is broken" alerts every five minutes the moment paper trading is switched on (those alerts are auto-silenced only while trading is off), and the go-live readiness gate would have waved a dead feed through as 100% healthy. This batch deleted the dead feed entirely (archived, with the system still compiling cleanly with it gone — proving nothing depended on it) and re-pointed all four monitors onto the real feed. The rebuilt alarm judges each market separately — crypto around the clock, xStock only while its market is open — won't cry wolf over one quiet thinly-traded name (it asks "is ANY name still ticking," not "is the slowest one stale"), stays silent when xStock is legitimately closed overnight/weekends, and has a short grace window right after the market reopens so it doesn't false-alarm before the first prices arrive; the go-live gate now correctly requires the real feed to actually be delivering data, not just be connected. The 30-second log spam is confirmed structurally gone (the code that produced it no longer exists — Langston independently watched four minutes of clean logs after deploy). The other half of a separate error-log finding (a scanner occasionally seeing a short pair list) got the logging needed to catch its cause, with the actual fix split into its own follow-up (P19-B6.9). The new alarm is dormant until paper trading turns on, so it's deploy-proven, structurally-proven, and covered by 27 automated tests, but not yet exercised live. Bench green (typecheck clean + 27 new tests), CI all-four-green, deployed staging restart#421, settings seeded. Langston reviewed and approved every step including an independent second-pass verification. Closes #301. Next: per the Phase-19 sequence.

**B-TEC-SELFHEAL shipped 2026-06-25 — the trailing-exit safety check that used to jam shut for hours now clears itself in about a minute ✅** (Claude Old; Langston-reviewed · `B_TEC_SELFHEAL_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md`). The part of the system that manages how trades exit has a deliberate safety rule: if its per-asset-class settings ever go stale, it refuses to act rather than act on bad numbers. That fail-safe is correct and was left unchanged. The problem was that once it tripped it stayed tripped until someone restarted the system — the staleness check ran *before* the step that refreshes the settings, so a stale reading blocked its own recovery, and there was no periodic refresh to break the latch. On top of that, the learning engine's exit loop had no per-trade guardrail, so a single stale-settings trade could abort the entire multi-asset-class cycle at its very first step — which blocked new trades from opening too. The real-world proof was the 06-22 xStock weekend reopen, where the safety check stuck for roughly 17 hours straight until a deploy restart. The fix moves the refresh trigger to run *before* the safety check (so a stale reading now schedules its own recovery and clears in about one cycle, while the fail-safe block itself stays byte-for-byte identical), and wraps the learning-engine exit loop so one bad trade is skipped instead of taking down the whole cycle. Both asset classes confirmed fresh after deploy with zero stuck-blocks; the real test is a scheduled check on the next xStock weekend reopen (06-29) to confirm it self-heals instead of cascading. Deployed (staging `5b9180120`, restart#417), all four automated checks green, Langston reviewed and approved at every step. Closes #349. Next: per the Phase-19 sequence.

**B-GOV-4 shipped 2026-06-26 — the governance watchdog that was crying wolf now only flags real gaps ✅** (Claude Old; Langston-reviewed · `B_GOV_4_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). The automated checker that watches whether each batch finished its paperwork had started firing false alarms in bursts — a review found roughly 22 of the last ~30 governance alerts weren't real. That's the exact failure Kyle worried about: if it cries wolf, everyone learns to ignore it and we lose the signal (it had already misled a triage into nearly re-doing paperwork that already existed). Three causes, all fixed. First, the checker treated a batch's name as "a batch being worked" if the name appeared *anywhere* in a commit message — so a commit that merely *mentioned* a finished batch's name made the checker re-grade it; now it only counts when the name *leads* the message. Second, it chopped multi-part names in half (a real batch named "…-SELFHEAL" was graded as a non-existent half-name that had no paperwork, firing seven false alarms); now it reads the whole name. Third, it checked a batch's paperwork the instant *any* record was touched — which could be seconds before the rest of the paperwork finished landing — so it flagged "missing" documents that were about to appear; now it waits until the batch's completion write-up exists (the last thing written when a batch truly closes), and any alert that still slips out early erases itself the moment the missing document shows up. A subtle extra fix: a finished batch's "graded-on" date is now locked to when its completion write-up was *first* committed, so even editing that old record later can't drag the batch back into grading. All 64 of the checker's logic tests pass (up from 48), the history-replay safety gate passes, and a live run against the real record confirms the three false-alarm sources are gone while genuine gaps still surface. While in there, one separate gap was found and written down for a later fix (a different family of batch names the checker can't see at all — the opposite problem, going silent rather than crying wolf), deliberately not bundled in to avoid scope creep. This is governance tooling only — nothing in the trading system changed. Langston reviewed the plan, the deep audit, and the actual code. Resolves #350 and #397. Next: per the Phase-19 sequence.

**B-TELEGRAM-DECOMM shipped 2026-07-02 — the old Telegram messaging machinery is retired for good ✅** (Claude Old; Kyle green-lit; Langston-reviewed · `B_TELEGRAM_DECOMM_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). The crew moved to Discord a week ago, with the old Telegram machinery left running as an emergency fallback until Discord proved itself. The scheduled readiness check fired on 07-02 and the week's evidence was clean: about fifteen hundred messages, zero errors, activity every single day. With Kyle's go-ahead the Telegram machinery was shut down and removed — not left dormant, per the never-leave-legacy-lingering rule, because an idle-but-present service is an accident waiting to be switched back on. Everything removed was first archived in two places, so it's recoverable if ever genuinely needed, and any attempt to switch the messaging back to Telegram now stops with a clear error message instead of silently talking to a dead service. Langston's own instruction files, which still described the Telegram world, were rewritten to match how the crew actually works now — he flagged that himself during review. One small piece of follow-up was deliberately left for a later window (a leftover Telegram code path inside the alert system on the staging server, currently switched off) so this cleanup wouldn't have to touch the trading application mid-implementation. Resolves #348.

**B-TELEGRAM-DECOMM-2 shipped 2026-07-02 — the last Telegram remnant is gone from the alert system ✅** (Claude Old; Kyle-directed; Langston-reviewed · `B_TELEGRAM_DECOMM_2_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). After the Telegram machinery was retired earlier the same day, one remnant remained: the alert system on the staging server still contained the code for posting alerts to Telegram — switched off since late June, but present, and per the never-leave-legacy rule a switched-off dead path is an accident waiting to happen. With Kyle's direction it was deleted outright: about 230 lines of dead code removed, leaving Discord as the alert system's single delivery path (which is exactly how it had already been operating for the past week). Langston confirmed from his own seat that nothing he depends on was lost — his alert feed runs through Discord plus his every-turn check of the alert queue — and reviewed the exact change before it shipped. The deployment was coordinated with New Claude so it wouldn't collide with his in-flight work. The proof was live: a real test alert was fired through the rebuilt path and arrived on Discord within seconds, engaged Langston automatically, and was resolved. A years-old tracked annoyance (a hardcoded personal chat ID) died with the deleted code. Resolves #351 and #107.

**reorg-B2.3 shipped 2026-06-27 — every strategy now has its own quality bar instead of one bar for all, set from its own track record ✅** (Claude New; Langston-reviewed · `P19_REORG_B2_3_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md`). The system was holding every strategy to a single quality bar (the reward had to be at least 2.5× the risk), but most strategies are built to aim for about 2× by design — so that one-size bar was quietly rejecting the bulk of perfectly good signals across the whole lineup. This batch gives each strategy its own bar, set just below that strategy's *own* typical reward-to-risk from four days of live measurement — and importantly, the stock-side strategies get their bars from stock-side data, never borrowed from crypto. Most bars came *down* (un-sticking strategies that were over-suppressed); the one exception is the system's strongest strategy, whose bar went *up* a notch because it can afford a stricter gate. Two design touches worth recording: the code that looks up a strategy's bar now refuses to compile if any part of the system asks for a bar without naming a strategy (so a future mistake can't quietly fall back to a loose default), and an unrecognized strategy name now gets the *strictest* bar plus an alarm, never a lenient one. While building it I found the system already had a strategy-name translator, so instead of writing a second one (a maintenance trap) I reused the existing one — Langston independently checked the live code and agreed that was the better call. His review also caught two real improvements I'd missed: that future-mistake compile-guard, and a subtle measurement-accuracy issue where a new statistic would have started mid-stream after the deploy and produced a misleading number for a later calibration phase — I added a companion counter so that phase can detect and skip the misaligned stretch. On the one judgment call — three strategies whose average reward is genuinely small — Kyle decided to keep them trading and judge them later on whether they actually make money (small reward with a high win-rate can still be profitable), rather than shelve them now on reward-size alone. Deployed, all four automated checks green, the four days of measurement history survived the restart intact, and the safety alarm shows zero unrecognized names in live signals. Langston reviewed and approved at every step (independent second-pass verification confirmed). Next: per the Phase-19 sequence (B6.8).

**P19-B6.8 shipped 2026-06-29 — the daily-loss warning levels are now settings the user controls, and a dead duplicate guardrails screen was cleared out ✅** (Claude New; Langston-reviewed · `P19_B6_8_COMPLETION_REPORT.md` · `PHASE_19_PLAN.md`). This one is a good example of checking the ground truth before building. The original plan assumed a bunch of the user's safety settings were still stuck in an old shared place and needed moving so paper and live could each have their own set. But when I traced the live code and database, that move had already happened in earlier work — the guardrails tab the user actually sees already saves paper's and live's safety limits separately and correctly (the database had distinct paper and live rows with different values). So the original premise was out of date, and most of the batch's imagined work was already done. What was genuinely missing: the two daily-loss *warning levels* (alert at 50% and 75% of your kill-switch limit) were running in the background and firing correctly, but the user couldn't actually see or change them in the tab. This batch surfaced them as two proper per-mode controls — so now you set your kill-switch limit AND the two warning levels, separately for paper and live, and the system enforces that they stay in sensible order (warning 1 below warning 2 below the hard stop). The failsafe behavior itself was already exactly as intended — alerts at 50/75, auto-shutdown at the limit, manual restart by you — confirmed, not rebuilt. While verifying on staging I caught a real bug: saving the new warning levels appeared to succeed but didn't actually stick, because the save function's field list had been missing those two fields — fixed and re-verified (set them, confirmed they persist for paper while live stays untouched, confirmed the order-check rejects bad values). I also deleted an old, dead duplicate guardrails screen that was still in the code but never shown to anyone (it would have confused future work), and scheduled the deeper cleanup of the matching old database table as its own follow-up (B6.10) since it's still wired into a couple of places that need migrating first. Deployed, all four build checks green, verified both in the live UI and the database. Langston reviewed and approved the plan, the code, and the staging result. Closes #302 (as already-satisfied plus the warning-level gap surfaced). Next: per the Phase-19 sequence (B6.9).

**P19-B6.8a shipped 2026-06-30 — the guardrails tab is now clearly labeled "Paper Guardrails" with a big bold "Paper Mode" header, and is locked to paper so the label can never lie ✅** (Claude New; Langston-reviewed · `P19_B6_8a_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). Kyle asked for two visible changes — make the tab button say "Paper Guardrails" instead of just "Guardrails," and replace the small "PAPER" badge next to the title with a big bold header that plainly says this is the paper-mode guardrails — with a separate "Live Guardrails" tab to be added later. The interesting part was underneath. The tab used to follow the app's global paper/live switch, and that switch starts on *live*. So if I'd just slapped a static "Paper" label on it, someone opening the page fresh would see their *live* safety limits sitting under a header that says "Paper" — which on a risk-limits screen is genuinely dangerous, not just untidy. The fix was to lock this tab to paper specifically (it now asks for paper's limits regardless of the global switch), which makes the "Paper" label always honest and matches where the system is going: Kyle confirmed that paper trading, live trading, and the always-on learning system now run side by side as separate systems, so each needs its own dedicated guardrails screen rather than one screen that flips. Building it this way makes the future "Live Guardrails" tab a one-line addition. The one tradeoff: until that live tab is added, the live limits aren't editable from the screen — which is fine for now because live trading isn't running and the live values stay safely saved in the database; I gave that live tab a concrete scheduled home (a Phase 21 to-do) so it doesn't get forgotten. Built, local build check clean, Langston reviewed and approved the approach and the code, deployed, all four build checks green, confirmed on the live staging screen. Next: per the Phase-19 sequence (B6.9).

**P19-B6.9 shipped 2026-06-30 — fixed a feed-health check that would have wrongly blocked the eventual switch to live trading, and confirmed (with evidence) that a suspected scanner problem isn't actually happening ✅** (Claude New; Langston-reviewed · `P19_B6_9_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). Two pre-live cleanup items were bundled here. The real fix: there's a safety check that, when we eventually turn on live trading, asks "has the market data feed been stable enough to trust?" — and it was counting *every* reconnection over the system's entire lifetime against a fixed budget. So the longer the system ran, the more it looked "unstable" on paper, until eventually it would fail that check even with a perfectly healthy feed at that moment. The fix makes it look at only the most recent hour instead, so a brief hiccup three weeks ago no longer counts against today; reusing the rolling-hour bookkeeping we already built last batch rather than inventing a second counter. Langston caught a subtle edge — right after a restart there's barely any recent data, so one blip would swing the number wildly — so it now honestly reports "still warming up, not ready yet" for the first half hour rather than guessing. The second item, the suspected "scanner only looks at 43 of 300 coins" problem, turned out not to be happening at all: the diagnostic we installed last batch to catch it has stayed completely silent, and the scanner is checking the full set every cycle. Rather than build a fix for something that isn't occurring, we parked it with that evidence and an automatic trigger that reopens it the instant the diagnostic ever fires (and that diagnostic also records the cause, so the eventual fix would be targeted, not guesswork). One thing we deliberately left for later and wrote down as a numbered to-do: the "healthy enough" bar is currently strict enough that a single reconnection in an hour would block go-live — whether to relax that is a tuning decision for when we actually approach live trading, not part of this fix. All 37 tests pass (including one that reproduces the exact old bug and proves it's gone), build checks green, deployed; Langston reviewed and approved the plan, the code, and confirmed the one load-bearing detail himself. Next: per the Phase-19 sequence (B7a).

**P19-B7.1 shipped 2026-06-30 — the system now picks which trade to take by the best reward-for-the-risk after costs, the same yardstick professional desks use, and on one scale that compares crypto and tokenized-stock opportunities fairly ✅** (Claude New; Langston-reviewed + Claude Old second-eyes · `P19_B7_1_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). This is the make-or-break of selection. The picker used to rank candidates by a "quality/confidence" score that was blind to both trading costs and how much you risk to make the trade — so a high-confidence but tiny-profit signal could out-rank a genuinely better one, and the profitability check would then just *bounce* the bad pick instead of the ranker promoting the good one in the first place. Kyle's direction here was important: don't design this from "what plumbing do we already have" — research what the trading industry actually uses, and if a better method needs new plumbing, build it; and the ranking must compare opportunities *across* asset classes on a common basis. So the design was grounded in established practice (two sourced surveys plus the senior reviewers all converged on the same answer): rank by the expected reward-per-unit-of-risk, net of costs — which is exactly what lets a crypto and a tokenized-stock candidate be compared on one scale. Underneath, the number used to rank is the system's *own* profitability figure reused directly, so the ranking and the final go/no-go check can never disagree, and it's computed without double-counting any of the data the system will later use to calibrate itself. The review was the process working as intended: Langston read the full change and caught three real issues — a telemetry counter recording in a spot that could have skewed a future tuning decision, an incompleteness in how I flagged "was this a real signal or a fallback default" (it missed one of the two fallback paths, fixed the robust way by reading the engine's own output instead of reproducing its logic from outside), and a statistics-weighting choice — all fixed and re-tested. The measurement layer that will later *prove* the new ranking actually predicts winners is built and tested now, though it only produces real numbers once paper trading is switched on (the next phase). Five calibration follow-ups are logged so none is forgotten. It's the default ranker but stays dormant until paper trading turns on. Type-checks clean, 20 new tests plus 51 related ones pass, all four build checks green, deployed to staging and verified healthy; Langston approved every step and did an independent second pass, Claude Old gave a second-eyes review. Next: per the Phase-19 sequence (B7.2, the maker/taker decision system).

**P19-B7.2 shipped 2026-07-01 — for every trade the system now decides whether to cross the market immediately or rest a patient order to save the fee, keeps the better of the two, and can finally open the crypto trades the fee wall used to refuse ✅** (Claude New; Langston-reviewed + Claude Old landmine-catch · `P19_B7_2_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). This is the structural crypto opener. On our current crypto fees, crossing the market immediately costs about 1.8% round-trip, and the honest profit check simply refuses most crypto trades on those economics — but resting a patient order costs roughly half, and that saving is what tips a genuinely-worthwhile trade back into "yes." So for each signal the system now prices *both* ways using the exact same profit math (only the fee/spread assumptions differ), and keeps whichever is better. The safety margin Kyle asked for lives in one place: a deliberately pessimistic assumption about how a patient order really fills — it assumes you fill at the worst moments and it never treats a missed fill as free (a missed fill is booked as a real opportunity cost, not zero). Momentum trades, where the move is already running, are pushed toward crossing now (a patient order gets left behind); mean-reversion trades, where you *want* to buy the dip, are allowed to rest. Crucially, the better-of-the-two number now flows through the whole chain — it's what the pick-the-best step ranks on and what the final go/no-go check uses — so a trade that's just barely unprofitable the expensive way can now open the patient way, while a badly-unprofitable one still gets refused. The patient-order waiting behavior is built too: it rests, fills only if the market actually trades through the price, and on a timeout either converts to crossing-now *only* if that's still profitable or cancels outright — it never opens a known loser. The review caught the sharp edges: Claude Old flagged a naming trap where two different "reward-to-risk" figures could have been silently swapped (one gross, one the real profit-weighted one), and Langston verified the math independently and had me structurally fix a fee-lookup so the two-way comparison never mixes sources. One small fee-lookup cleanup was split into its own follow-up to keep this batch clean; the haircut starts deliberately tight and is a dial we loosen later once real fills give us data. Type-checks clean, 13 new tests pass (including the key ones: a just-barely-unprofitable trade opens patiently, a badly-unprofitable one is still refused), all four build checks green, deployed to staging and verified healthy (the new decision knobs and columns confirmed live in the database); nothing is switched on yet — paper-mode active trading stays off until a later step turns it on. Langston approved every step and did an independent second pass on staging. Next: per the Phase-19 sequence (the crypto gate-10 lifecycle proof, then B8 — the paper-trading switch-on).

**P19-B7.2b shipped 2026-07-01 — the cross-vs-rest decision now runs in the learning system too, every trade record shows which fee mode its entry used, and the earlier wrong-stage code was cleaned out ✅** (Claude New; Langston-reviewed + Claude Old second-eyes · `P19_B7_2b_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). This completes the shared decision and fixes a design mistake. Three things landed. First, the same cross-vs-rest decision the practice-trading path uses now also runs inside the learning system (the one that has been quietly simulating trades for months), so both learn from the same economics — and on the active path the decision was moved to sit just before the quality filter rather than inside it, keeping that filter free of calculations as intended. Second, a design correction: the earlier version tried to *work* a patient order while a candidate was still sitting in the waiting list, which was the wrong place — a waiting candidate should only carry a *decision*; the actual order goes out only when it's chosen to trade. So that in-the-waiting-list order machinery was removed and properly logged, and the waiting list now simply re-decides cross-vs-rest each cycle on fresh prices. Third, every trade record across both systems — the waiting list, the open trades, and the closed trades — now carries which fee mode the entry used (cross-now or rest-patiently) plus the actual fee rate, and that shows as a new "Entry Fee Mode" column on all four of those screens; anything from before this change simply shows a dash instead of a made-up value. The review worked as intended: Langston read the whole change line-by-line, verified it independently against the live system, and caught one subtle consistency point — when a waiting candidate got re-evaluated it was looking up its strategy under a slightly different name than the original decision used, which could have made it pick a different fee mode for one strategy; traced, confirmed real, and fixed so both paths use the exact same canonical name. It's confirmed working end-to-end on the live learning screen: brand-new crypto trades correctly show "Taker (0.80%)" and older ones show the dash — Langston independently queried the database and found exactly the five new trades stamped, the rest blank, proving the whole chain. Type-checks clean, all tests pass, all four build checks green, deployed to staging and verified healthy (Langston independently checked the database columns and the deploy); nothing is switched on yet — the practice-trading and waiting-list screens can't fill with rows until paper trading turns on (a later step), so those were confirmed to use the same shared column but exercised at that point. Next: build the patient-order fill behavior for both systems (B7.2c, which Kyle asked to build now not defer), then the crypto lifecycle proof and B8 — the paper-trading switch-on.

**P19-B7.2c shipped 2026-07-02 — patient orders now actually wait to be filled, unfilled ones are dropped after an hour and shown honestly, and the learning system opens both versions of every trade to compare ✅** (Claude New; Langston-reviewed · `P19_B7_2c_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). Kyle simplified the design mid-batch and the simple version is what shipped: when the system picks the patient (rest-and-wait) way to enter a trade, that trade now sits in the open-trades list marked PENDING, holding one of the limited trade slots, until the real market price actually reaches its asking price — only then does it fill, at exactly that price with the cheaper fee. If a full hour passes without a fill, the order is simply dropped — no second-guessing, no converting it into something else (Kyle: "if it times out, it's dropped"). A dropped order still shows up in the closed-trades list labeled "Never filled — dropped," so there's an honest record that a trade was attempted and never happened — but it is deliberately kept OUT of the win-rate, profit, and learning numbers (a trade that never existed can't be a win or a loss), while still counting toward the "how often do patient orders actually fill" statistic we'll need later. If the market is already past the asking price the moment the order would be placed, the system checks whether the trade was also profitable the immediate way and, if so, just takes it immediately instead. And per Kyle's ask, the learning system now opens BOTH versions of every such trade — the patient one and the immediate one — as a tagged pair, so we accumulate side-by-side data on which entry style actually wins; the tagged twin is fenced off from every capacity limit and every statistic so it can never distort the real numbers, and a single switch can turn twinning off without a redeploy. Langston reviewed the full change line-by-line, independently verified the risky spots (tags surviving a restart, no phantom fees on dropped orders), and caught one real hazard — a garbage price reading of zero could have falsely filled an order — which was fixed before anything shipped. All checks green, deployed, database verified, and the new screens confirmed rendering live. The one honest caveat: the very first pending order, first twin pair, and first hour-timeout drop hadn't yet occurred naturally at close (the learning system produces them as new signals arrive; a drop takes an hour by definition), so first-occurrence evidence follows as a soak note. Next: the small fee-bookkeeping consolidation (B7.2a), then the naming cleanup, the crypto lifecycle proof, and B8 — the paper-trading switch-on.

**P19-B7.2a shipped 2026-07-02 — the fee now comes from exactly one place, everywhere ✅** (Claude New; Langston-reviewed · `P19_B7_2a_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). The system had two separate roads to the same fee number: the official database-governed lookup, and a per-pair cache that quietly kept its own copy — complete with its own duplicate lookup code, a hardcoded safety cap that could have silently overridden the governed fee if the account's tier ever changed enough, and a five-minute staleness window. Nothing was wrong in practice (both roads produced the same number today), but that's exactly the kind of structural duplication that turns into a silent money-math bug later. Now the fee is never stored in the cache at all — every part of the system asks the one official source at the moment it needs the number, matched to the right asset class for that spot in the code, and the type system itself enforces it (any leftover reader of the old copy fails to even compile). The cache goes back to doing only what it was built for: per-pair measured market friction. The review process earned its keep twice here — Langston caught four places reading the fee off the cache through a side door my first survey missed (two of them feeding real friction scoring, not just display), and my counter-survey of his earlier question found four consumers of the cache's fee statistic that would have broken silently, including the cost-drift alarm — which, as a bonus, is now strictly more truthful: it only fires when the fee actually changes in the database, never from a cache artifact. New tests pin all of it, including one that deliberately makes the two asset classes' fees differ and fails if any scoring site ever flattens them together. All checks green, deployed, and proven live: all 642 cached pairs serving exactly the official fee. Next: the naming cleanup, the crypto lifecycle proof, and B8 — the paper-trading switch-on.

**P19-B7.2d shipped 2026-07-03 — the fee-choice machinery now also runs for xStock simulated trades, closing the gap Kyle spotted ✅** (Claude New; Langston-reviewed · `P19_B7_2d_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). Kyle noticed that the open simulated xStock trades all showed a dash in the Entry Fee Mode column — and the cause was real: the xStock side of the learning system opens its trades through its own doorway, and the cross-vs-rest fee decision added earlier had only been wired into the crypto doorway. The completion claim from that earlier batch said the learning system was wired when only half of it was — both Claude New and Langston owned that miss, and a lesson entry now requires such claims to name exactly which lane they cover. This batch wires the same shared decision into the xStock doorway: every evaluated xStock signal now weighs both entry styles and is judged on the better of the two, new opens are stamped with their fee mode so the column fills in for new trades (old rows keep the honest dash), patient-style entries wait as PENDING for a real fill or get dropped after an hour, and each trade opens with its tagged comparison twin — all through the exact same shared machinery crypto uses, so the two can never drift apart. Getting there required carefully moving the twin-opening code out of crypto's live hot path into a shared home — a path that had been deliberately locked against modification — so Langston formally lifted that lock for just this piece, demanded proof the move changed nothing about crypto's behavior on both the "opens a twin" and "correctly skips" sides, and then independently verified the riskiest assumption himself against the live code before approving. A suspected weekend hazard (a waiting order being silently converted into an open position over the market-closed boundary) was investigated and proven not to exist, with warning notes added so no future change can quietly create it. All checks green, deployed, and verified live: the decision demonstrably running on xStock signals within minutes, the resting ZRO order surviving a restart intact, and all the control knobs confirmed in the database. Next: the naming cleanup (picking B-RENAME back up), the crypto lifecycle proof, and B8 — the paper-trading switch-on.

**P19-B-RENAME shipped 2026-07-03 — every misleading "paper" name in the active trading path now tells the truth ✅** (Claude New; double-blind inventory with Claude Old; Langston-gated at every step · `P19_B_RENAME_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). The whole three-wave cleanup ran overnight. Wave 1 deleted the dead weight: the old test harness (entirely — the piece we planned to rescue turned out to feed endpoints nothing ever called), the obsolete command-line scripts, and the empty Walter-era report tables Kyle ruled on. Wave 2 renamed the code: the trading engine and its seven sibling files, their exported names, the web addresses, the internal signal names, and the configuration keys — with one stored data label deliberately left untouched forever (renaming it would corrupt historical learning data; a guard test now makes that protection permanent). Wave 3 renamed the four database tables, one at a time, each moving with every piece of code that touches it in a single atomic step, the highest-traffic table going last with its own isolated deploy and soak. The proof: the type system and full test suite green at every step, and within five minutes of the final deploy the renamed system filled a resting order at its exact limit and closed two trades cleanly. One valuable discovery surfaced during verification: the closed-trades table turns out to have never received the learning-system trades an old design document promised — that path was never wired — so its emptiness is now an asserted tripwire while trading is off, and the real decision (wire it or correct the document) has a named home at the switch-on batch. One CI stumble (fresh test databases are born already renamed, so the rename instructions had to learn to skip themselves) was fixed forward within minutes. Next: the crypto lifecycle proof and B8 — the paper-trading switch-on.

**P19-B8.1 shipped 2026-07-04 — the trading screens reorganized into three mode pages ✅** (Claude New; Kyle-led design discussion → Langston consensus incl. a commissioned field-research pass · `P19_B8_1_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). The single Trading page is gone; in its place: Live Trading (honestly dormant until Phase 21), Paper Trading (with the start/stop control moved INTO the page — the toggle no longer follows you around the top bar), and Virtual Simulations (the VTS's own home — its open/closed trade tables and both filter-diagnostics tabs moved there from the Machine Learning page, which slimmed down to its true calibration job). All three pages are one shared shell fed by per-mode tab manifests, so Paper and Live can never drift apart. The Pattern Scanning tab and the whole Filter Insights surface were deleted to full standard — and the pre-cut consumer trace earned its keep by catching that a piece originally slated for deletion actually feeds the SQE and position sizing (kept). Four broken diagnostics counters were fixed at their true sources, headlined by a cache-refresh race that had been silently breaking configuration reads roughly hourly for weeks — root-caused from live evidence and closed structurally. Every page and tab was walked on staging per Kyle's full-visual-audit requirement, and Langston independently reproduced the proofs on the box. The batch also locked the whole paper-balance design (Kraken-mirror start, automatic physics-based re-anchor, snap-to-live at launch, the dollar-agnostic learning fence) — that build is next as B8.2.

**P19-B8.2 shipped 2026-07-05 — the balance policy is real: paper starts at the actual Kraken balance, ghosts are dead, and the re-anchor machinery is proven ✅** (Claude New; Langston-gated at every step, same-day scope→deploy · `P19_B8_2_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). Starting a new paper session now fetches the real Kraken account balance and shows it read-only for confirmation — there is no input field, and if Kraken can't be reached the start refuses rather than inventing a number (Continue never calls Kraken, so an outage never blocks resuming).
