Every made-up balance was deleted: the seven known ghost defaults, plus two the earlier sweeps missed — hardcoded $10,000 denominators inside the engine's own exposure and drawdown safety math, which at the real balance understated exposure eleven-fold against the heat ceilings. The database ghosts died too: the leftover $25,000 scenario row and an entire abandoned duplicate balance table. Every balance re-base is now an auditable anchor event in an append-only ledger, and each trade is stamped at open with the balance context it opened under so future calibration can segment honestly across balance eras. The batch's best catch came from the first live test: the real account holds zero plain dollars — the entire balance is USDC — so the strict counting rule was revised on live evidence to count exactly the currencies the trading universe itself accepts. The re-anchor machinery was proven end-to-end on the live-mode record (one ledger entry, learning data byte-identical before and after), and the no-dollars-in-decision-logic fence now runs as a self-proving automated check on every build — with the sweep confirming the live decision path already had zero dollar thresholds, so nothing needed Kyle's sign-off. Two loose ends got numbered homes: the stablecoin question for live launch, and a long-broken legacy settings write path for the already-scheduled cleanup batch. Next: B8.3 dashboards, then B8.4 — the switch-on.

**P19-B8.3 shipped 2026-07-06 — every mode page opens on its own Dashboard, and every number on it is honest ✅** (Claude New; Langston-gated Step-1 through Step-8, same-day scope→close · `P19_B8_3_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). Paper Trading, Live Trading, and Virtual Simulations each now land on a Dashboard tab built on the familiar widget layout Kyle asked for — portfolio value, calendar earnings (today / this week / this month), win rate with the raw counts beside every percentage, averages and edge measures, a balance-growth-over-time chart that marks every balance re-base, and breakdowns by asset class and by strategy. The paper page even shows the real Kraken account balance beside the simulated one, live. Two long-standing dishonest numbers died at the root: the profit percentage that always read zero now divides by the real starting balance from the balance-policy work, and a profit measure that displayed "0.00" on an all-wins window (reading like the worst day instead of the best) now says "∞ (no losses)". The filter-diagnostics tabs became mode-honest: on the VTS page the gate table shows Dropped vs Tagged (tagged setups still simulate — that's the learning design), while on Paper/Live it shows one true Rejected total that provably equals the sum of its reason columns — verified adding up exactly on live data — plus a banner explaining that one scanner feeds all modes with per-mode thresholds, and each mode's own pipeline-tail readout. The metrics strip moved from the top bar onto the trading pages with clear labels for the three different balance meanings. And the scare that kicked this off — tables showing zero trades when trades existed — can't recur silently: every data fetch that fails now says so on screen instead of rendering an empty table. Langston verified the endpoints himself on the box and signed the second pass. Three small residuals have numbered homes in the next batch (B8.3b), which builds the per-mode funnel counters; then B8.4 — the switch-on.

**P19-B8.3b shipped 2026-07-07 — the dashboard fast-follow: the internal-simulator numbers no longer bleed onto the trading pages, a mislabeled dead counter is gone, and the headline total is now after-fees ✅** (Claude New; Langston Step-1 through Step-8, same-day scope→close · `P19_B8_3b_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). The Paper and Live filter-diagnostics tabs used to show the always-on learning engine's own processing numbers (how many signals it evaluated, how many trades it opened) mixed in with the shared market-scan feed — which read as if paper trading were already active when it isn't. Those blocks are now hidden on the Paper and Live pages, replaced by an honest "this fills in when active trading is switched on" note, while the Virtual Simulations page keeps showing them (that's where they belong). The architectural insight that made this clean: the market scanner is one shared feed that automatically *becomes* the active-trading funnel the moment trading is switched on, so nothing new had to be built — just shown honestly per page. A mislabeled internal counter that was quietly sent to the browser but never actually displayed was removed entirely. The headline profit figure now uses the same after-fees basis as the per-category breakdown, so the two always add up (previously the headline used the before-fees number). And the balance chart now draws a flat line at your real balance during a quiet stretch instead of claiming "no history." The most useful moment came at the visual-check step: the on-screen walk-through caught a *second* internal-numbers block that the code review couldn't have seen (a review only shows what changed, and this block was one I'd left unchanged by omission) — fixed and re-verified on the spot. Next: the counts-restore follow-up (B8.3c), then B8.4 — the switch-on.

**P19-B8.3c shipped 2026-07-07 — the open/closed trade counts are back atop the trade tabs ✅** (Claude New; Kyle-directed via Claude Old, who traced the cause; Langston-reviewed · `P19_B8_3c_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). Kyle noticed the little count that used to sit at the top of each trade tab (how many open trades, how many closed) had gone missing — it was dropped when the trade tables were pulled out of the old Machine Learning page and the trading pages were split apart back in B8.1. Restored: the two Virtual Simulations tabs and the shared open-trades panel (which Paper and Live both use) now show their count again. A small correct-by-construction detail: each count is simply the number of rows in the table right below it, so the header and the list can never disagree. And a modest cleanup along the way — the closed-trades tab on Paper and Live turned out to have never lost its count (it still showed "N total trades"), so that one was left alone rather than bolting on a redundant second number. Purely visual, no behavior change. Next: B8.4 — the Filter-Diagnostics instrumentation (the switch-on was renumbered to B8.5).

**P19-B8.4 shipped 2026-07-08 — the Paper and Live diagnostic tabs are wired to show each mode's own trade-idea funnel, for both crypto and xStock ✅** (Claude New; Langston-reviewed · `P19_B8_4b_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). Kyle renumbered the plan so B8.4 is now this instrumentation batch and the actual switch-on became B8.5. Part-1 (shipped earlier) made each diagnostic tab show only its own scanner stage and stop borrowing crypto's numbers on the xStock tab. Part-2 (this) wired the counting into the live trading pipeline: how many trade ideas the strategies produce, where each gets filtered out (before and after the quality check, as separate honest stages), how the quality check itself decides gate-by-gate, and what happens during each ready-to-buy refresh, including promotions — all for both crypto and xStock. A count that sits *before* an idea is even formed (strategies set aside by the family filter) now has its own row above the funnel, so the numbers always add up. Everything is connected but reads zero until paper-mode active trading turns on at B8.5 — the wiring lands now, the live data first flows then. Langston reviewed and independently re-verified on staging; the diagnostic tabs render the dormant "awaiting activation" state cleanly on Paper, Live, and both asset classes. One small error-row edge case is homed to B8.5. Next: B8.5 — the switch-on.

**B-STORAGE-HARDEN Wave A shipped 2026-07-08 — the "keep-forever" archive is switched on, and the system now raises real alarms if storage backups silently fail ✅** (Claude Old; Kyle-authorized autonomous run; Langston-reviewed · `B_STORAGE_HARDEN_WAVE_A_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). Triggered by Kyle's Supabase disk-expansion email (135→202 GB). Two safety pieces landed. First, the permanent bottom layer of data storage — the cheap Backblaze "keep-forever" archive that was built months ago but never actually turned on — is now active, so the oldest data finally has a real place to land and never gets deleted. It was proven for real by moving one small old file into it and reading it back out with a matching fingerprint. That real test-move immediately flushed out a hidden bug in the never-before-run archive-recording step (which would have made every real archive attempt fail); it was fixed the same run, with no data at risk because the original always stays put until the copy is confirmed. Second, the disk-usage monitor — which previously noticed the disk filling but never told anyone — now raises a real alert, and a new daily watchdog alerts if any nightly storage backup silently stops running. Still open in later waves: extending that same "never delete" protection to the trading-analysis data currently dropped every 90 days (the 90-day delete stays PAUSED until then). Next for this batch: Wave C (that trading-analysis-data protection).

**P19-B8.4c shipped 2026-07-08 — the six Filter-Diagnostics tabs are now standardized, and the scanner box tells the truth ✅** (Claude New; Kyle UI feedback; Langston-reviewed · `P19_B8_4c_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). The scanner box at the top of every diagnostics tab was showing a single mislabeled "capacity" number; it's now split into two honest ones — a per-cycle target (how many pairs each scan aims to cover: 300 for crypto, 75 for xStock) and a live total universe (the full list it rotates through: ~1,500 for crypto, ~481 for xStock, both pulled live rather than hard-coded). Every table across all six tabs is now the same width (the xStock scanner no longer stretches full-screen), and the Paper and Live tabs now mirror the three-table layout of the Virtual Simulations tabs — shown clearly as "awaiting activation" until the switch-on, never a bare zero. Each table got a bigger bold title with its own color filling the header bar (blue, purple, teal), and on a phone each table scrolls sideways on its own with the label column frozen in place. Along the way two superseded display pieces were deleted cleanly (archived, with their behind-the-scenes data collectors kept for the switch-on). The best moment was in review: Langston flagged the crypto universe figure as not actually wired — but he'd checked the wrong data path; a line-by-line trace showed it flows correctly through the shared feed, he verified it himself on the server, and the live staging walk confirmed the crypto tabs really do show 1,509. Next: B8.5 — the switch-on.

**B-STORAGE-HARDEN Wave C shipped 2026-07-08 — the trading-analysis data is now kept forever instead of deleted every 90 days ✅** (Claude Old; Kyle-authorized autonomous; Langston-reviewed · `B_STORAGE_HARDEN_WAVE_C_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). This is the core fix the whole batch was for. The five tables that record how the system scans, evaluates, and exits — the raw material for future calibration and learning — were being deleted outright every 90 days, which broke Kyle's "we never throw data away" rule (an oversight from when that data layer was first built). Now all five ride the exact same proven "move, don't delete" pipeline as the market-data archives: at 90 days each month's data is copied to cheap warm storage (verified intact before the original is removed), then later rolled down to the permanent keep-forever archive — nothing is lost, and the disk footprint is unchanged. The old delete-only job is retired. I proved the whole path end-to-end on the smallest table (copied a real month out, read it back with a matching fingerprint, rolled it to the permanent archive, and restored it from there), added a safety lock so the nightly job can't overlap itself once the big analysis table starts archiving in the fall, and scheduled a check for that first real run. Langston reviewed every step. Still keeping for Kyle's confirmation at close: keeping the raw scan-history by archiving it (rather than deleting) — the recommendation is yes. What's left in the batch: Wave D (reducing how much xStock price data we capture in the first place — a separate efficiency lever, not a data-loss issue).

**B-GOV-INTEGRITY-0 shipped 2026-07-10 (core) — the automatic governance check can no longer go blind or silently fall behind** (Claude New; Kyle governance-integrity directive; Langston-reviewed - B_GOV_INTEGRITY_0_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). The check had spent two weeks grading each batch's paperwork against a frozen, weeks-old copy of the rulebook, so it raised false alarms and could miss real gaps. Three things changed and every one was verified running for real: it now always reads the current rulebook and stops with a loud alarm if it ever cannot read it at all; it raises a visible warning the moment its own running copy falls behind the approved one, so it can never again drift unnoticed; and every alarm it clears now carries a checkable proof of why, which the alert system stores and which genuinely traces back to exactly what the check looked at. This interlocks with the Claude Old alert-record work: the checker supplies the proof, the alert system stores and enforces it. One convenience piece remains (owned, dated) - making future updates reach the check's machine automatically instead of by hand; the new fall-behind warning already makes any lag instantly visible in the meantime.

**B-DISCORD-INBOUND-LIVENESS shipped 2026-07-13 — the message feed can no longer die silently; a dead feed now shouts and heals itself** (Claude Old; Langston Step-1/2/4/8 ALL PASSED, CLOSED 2026-07-13 — B_DISCORD_INBOUND_LIVENESS_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). The connection that delivers Discord messages to the AI sessions had twice died while looking perfectly healthy — briefly on July 11 and for ~21 hours on July 12 — leaving both sessions silently deaf. The fix is a watchdog on both bridges that watches whether messages are TRULY still arriving (via Discord's own ~40-second keep-alive, so it works even on a quiet channel), not just whether the program is running. When the feed goes stale it shouts — posts a phone alert down the SENDING path, which stays alive even when receiving is dead — then restarts itself to reconnect, and catches up any messages missed during the outage. A live kill-test proved the whole chain: the feed was deliberately severed, the watchdog caught it in ~165 seconds, alerted, restarted, waited patiently while the outage continued (instead of giving up and latching itself permanently off — a trap Langston caught in review), then reconnected within 8 seconds and recovered. Now live on both the CC and Langston bridges. Closes RUNNING_ISSUES #462.

**B-LANGSTON-QUEUE-2 shipped 2026-07-11 — the review-queue that was silently losing Langston's work is repaired and hardened, on Kyle's "do it now" order** (Claude Old; Langston Step-4 + box-verified - B_LANGSTON_QUEUE_2_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). The list that tracks Langston's reviews had a cluster of real defects, all now fixed and live: it could spawn a hidden second copy of itself from a harmless "--help" (the 17-day ghost that doubled every reply) — now a hard guard refuses a second copy; it chopped his review summaries at 500 characters so a re-fed review arrived truncated and he couldn't finish it — now they're kept whole; it silently deleted finished entries when full — now it moves them to a keep-forever archive instead, the same never-delete rule we use on the database, and a test proves the "STOP, the error is live" message survives; it dropped a verdict that named an item it couldn't find — now that fails loudly and is recoverable; two programs could overwrite each other's edits to the file — now every edit takes a lock; and a late, contradictory verdict on an already-settled item can no longer quietly overwrite the original. All six deployed to the live bridge and verified end-to-end (Langston's own replies now travel the fixed path), with Langston independently re-reading each fix at the machine. Also landed: Kyle's request that governance-checker alerts say "GOVERNANCE CHECK ISSUE" instead of "SYSTEM ALERT" so he can tell them apart at a glance. The whole governance-repair arc — the checker, the alert record, and the review queue — is now closed and running.

**B-GOV-ORPHAN-CLASS shipped 2026-07-13 — the automatic governance check now stops re-flagging items that were already handled** (Claude New; Kyle "fix the old issues + do not keep making the same mistake" directive; Langston Step-1/2/4/8 all reviewed - B_GOV_ORPHAN_CLASS_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). The recurring annoyance was already-closed items re-appearing on the check list. Two root causes, both fixed and verified running for real: when a batch size-label is confirmed by hand the check simply never READ that confirmation, so it kept re-flagging the batch — now it reads it, and a confirmed batch stays closed; and the check internal working-list never cleaned itself against the real record, so stale flags piled up — now it self-cleans every cycle against the authoritative record and cannot re-accumulate. A related sweep also clears aged-out label-flags once the label is confirmed, and a small cross-platform path bug is fixed. On the machine, the four items that kept re-appearing now stay closed and the list cleaned itself; one leftover flag on a sub-batch documented under its parent was confirmed not-a-bug and given a one-line covered-under-the-parent note. Two tracked issues (#497, #352) closed. Langston verified every step at the source; passed its own 75-check test suite + all four automated checks. Along the way I made a reading mistake — claimed something was not there from a read that had quietly failed — owned it, corrected it, and built a permanent guard that blocks me from that exact mistake going forward.

**B-RANKING-COMPONENT-CAPTURE shipped 2026-07-22 — the record we keep to judge our own trade picks had three of its four inputs blank on every row ✅** (Claude Old; Langston Step-4 approved after independently re-deriving the evidence himself - B_RANKING_COMPONENT_CAPTURE_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). The system keeps a "did we pick the best one?" record for every promotion cycle — the raw material for later judging whether our selection is any good. It was storing **nothing** for three of the four numbers that drive that decision, on all 14,232 rows collected so far, while dutifully storing the fourth. So a dataset built specifically to grade our choices could not see what the choices were made from. The cause was a mismatch nobody had noticed: the recorder was reading three dedicated database columns that had been empty since the day they were created — the code that files a new signal never filled them in — while the live system kept the real values somewhere else entirely. The fix points the recorder at where the values actually live, fills them in at signal creation so brand-new rows are complete, and deletes the three permanently-empty columns rather than leaving them looking wired. A stale code comment claiming the whole feature was "dormant, no rows" — flatly untrue at 14,232 rows — was corrected in the same change, because it would have told the next person to delete a working capability. Trading behaviour was never affected: decisions were always made on the live values, never the blanks. **Three honest caveats.** The proof that new rows now record real numbers has NOT been observed — the recorder only runs when a trade is promoted, and with every slot full none has run since; that was stated rather than claimed. A fourth number was found to be silently recording *confidence* under the *hybrid score* name, which is worse than a blank because it looks valid; that substitution was removed too, with absent now honestly staying absent. And the batch's paperwork was written after the fact, only once the automatic governance check caught seven missing documents — recorded as a process failure rather than quietly backfilled.

**B-REGIME-REFRESH-PIPE shipped 2026-07-21 — the queue's live re-check now reads a real, current read of market conditions for every waiting signal, and a gate that had been dead for six months is finally alive ✅** (Claude Old; Kyle-delegated to completion with Langston, urgent — live trading, most of the waiting queue was being rejected; Langston Step-1 through Step-4 all reviewed, census independently re-derived - B_REGIME_REFRESH_PIPE_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). The system re-checks each waiting signal every 30 seconds to keep it honest, and part of that check reads how the market regime looks right now. But the engine that computes regime only ever did so for the fresh scanner survivors — and the scanner deliberately skips any pair that's already in the queue or already held, so the moment a signal joined the queue its regime read went stale and the re-check found nothing to read. The result on the live system: 54 of 55 waiting signals were being thrown out every cycle, not because they were bad but because the re-check had no current data. Digging into why, the regime part of that re-check turned out to have been running on two frozen placeholder numbers ever since the re-check was first built — months before the regime engine even existed — so this particular safety gate had never once been able to do its job in its entire history. The fix gives the re-check its own direct line to the regime engine: a new, side-effect-free calculation that reads current price movement and trend for exactly those waiting pairs, without disturbing anything else. It went live and was verified working for both crypto and tokenized stocks — the mass rejection went from most of the queue to zero, with a real spread of live regime values flowing on both sides. Two honest misses are on the record: a first version of a data-sufficiency guard was too strict and briefly blocked every tokenized-stock signal (caught from the live data and fixed); and after that fix I nearly told Kyle a separate stale-data problem was still blocking tokenized stocks — I distrusted the reading, added a diagnostic, re-measured on a warmed-up system, and found I'd been wrong, before it reached him. Kyle then asked whether the tokenized-stock side has enough price history over the weekend when the market is closed; verified against the live data (487 of 488 names hold enough stored history) that it rides the existing weekend snapshot, with a dated watch armed to confirm it reads fresh bars at the first real Sunday reopen.

**P19-B8.12 shipped 2026-07-19 — the scanner's ideal/rotational pool marking now reaches the active path, and Kyle's correction is the reason it exists** (Claude New; Langston conditions discharged; deployed and verified same night). One batch earlier we had hidden the Pool column on the paper tabs, reasoning it was a virtual-simulation-only concept with no active-path equivalent. Kyle challenged that from memory and was right: the scanner marks every single pair it collects with which pool it came from, and that marking was being silently discarded at one narrow hand-off — the third time this exact class of quiet field-loss has been found in the pipeline, after the regime-strength score and the pattern name. The marking now survives to the trade tables, the hidden column is restored rather than left stubbed, and the dashboard gained the hover explanations and the window indicator Kyle asked for. Verification produced a clean before-and-after: every signal created before the deploy carries no marking, every one created after carries it. The first thing that restored visibility showed was a question rather than a confirmation — the new signals came entirely from the rotational pool and none from the ideal pool, which is the reverse of the intended design. That was deliberately recorded as an observation with a stated evidence bar rather than acted on, since a Sunday inside the xStock shutdown is a plausible innocent explanation and the standing rule is that an apparent bug is a hypothesis, not a verdict.

**P19-B8.11 shipped 2026-07-19 — the dashboard's earnings now count rolling windows, and the permanently-blank pool column is off the paper tabs** (Claude New; Kyle's dashboard-review directives, same-day turnaround; Langston GO with the rename completeness independently re-verified). The Earnings card had been counting calendar buckets — since midnight, since Sunday, since the 1st — which made the numbers lurch whenever a boundary reset; it now counts true rolling windows (last 24 hours, last 7 days, last 30 days), matching the standing rule that rolling windows beat calendar snapshots, with the old calculator renamed away entirely rather than aliased. And the Pool (I/R) column — a VTS-only concept with no active-path equivalent that Kyle had flagged twice as a mystery blank — is hidden on the paper tabs while the VTS tabs keep it, returning only if the active pipeline ever gains that axis. Shipped alongside the full plain-language answer round on what every dashboard metric means, including the honest headline that the −1.77R average and the 154% fee drag are the fee wall showing up in realized results, and that a negative ranking score is structurally unopenable in live mode.

**P19-B8.10 shipped 2026-07-18 — the trade tables stop lying: blank columns now captured at signal birth, the Rank column's hidden retired-metric leak fixed, and the Phase-8 leftover tables fully purged** (Claude New; Kyle's evening directive round; Langston Step-1/2 PROCEED + Step-4 two rounds to APPROVED with one archive-integrity catch on record - P19_B8_10_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). Kyle's list of blank columns on the paper Open/Closed tabs — regime, global regime, pair and global friction, pair and global DBS, the pattern name, closed-row liquidity — all traced to one cause: the active pipeline never wrote those values down at signal creation, while the VTS always has. The fix captures each one at genesis from the exact same sources the VTS reads, with hard honesty rules: a value with no honest source stays absent, nothing is fabricated, and no history is backfilled — proven live four minutes after deploy when the first new signal carried its regime, its pattern name (a PATTERN signal finally naming INSIDE_BAR — the same hand-off point that had dropped the DBS before the previous day's fix), both frictions, both DBS readings, and its liquidity. The Rank column investigation found the column was showing a RETIRED quality score leaking through a hidden fallback rather than the ranking score that decides promotion; the fallback is removed, and open trades now show the actual score each trade won its slot with, under its own "Promote R" label so it can never be confused with the live queue value — and the dead legacy ranking machinery behind the leak (two functions nothing calls, parked for deletion since June) was deleted outright, discharging that June decision. The old tables below Ready-to-Buy (execution metrics + signal lifecycle audit, November-2025 era) were purged completely — screen, service, endpoints, and every telemetry write — with one load-bearing survivor relocated: the tiny function that mints every signal's ID, moved verbatim with a format-pinning test. Display round-out: Ready-to-Buy gets the frozen header and frozen Rank/Symbol columns, Slot sits beside Symbol, the perpetual "pending" under Global DBS becomes an honest blank, Regime Wt leaves the closed table (its constant 0.50 filed into the strategy-weighting investigation), and the Edge column feeds from the honest post-friction expected edge. Langston's review earned its keep twice: he caught the deletion archive over-collecting five still-live functions (regenerated programmatically with an automated leak check), and demanded proof the new promote-score actually persists (cited end-to-end, confirmed against a live database row). Entry-slip correctness is under Claude Old's investigation per Kyle's hand-off.

**P19-B8.7 Step-9 shipped 2026-07-17 — one set of trading tables everywhere, and the review Kyle ordered found a signal lane that had been silently dead for months** (Claude New; Kyle's layout-identity directive + his morning missing-DBS question; Langston GO after a three-round citation-discipline iteration + independent Step-8 runtime pass - P19_B8_7_STEP9_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). The paper Open and Closed Trades tabs now render through the exact same table components the VTS uses, fed by a small tested translation layer — same columns, same order, frozen symbol column and header row, with the paper extras (slot, price-feed source, manual close) appended and switched off for VTS. The old 1,376-line paper table file was deleted outright, taking with it a fantasy-fee bug: it had been recomputing on-screen P/L between server updates using fee constants hardcoded two eras ago, far below real Kraken fees. The Costs column became a five-way split (entry fee, entry slippage, exit fee, exit slippage, total) populated from real captured ingredients — which exposed that VTS only ever saved a blended total, now fixed going forward with older rows honestly dashed — and that dig surfaced a second hardcoded number: the xStock evaluation lane was using crypto's spread constant instead of the measured per-symbol spread the system already collects, making xStock virtual trades look slightly cheaper than reality; fixed, admission correspondingly tightens. **The headline: Kyle's question about two signals missing their DBS unraveled a dead lane.** The pattern pool's intake was discarding the DBS the scanner had computed; the signal pipeline feeds regime classification from that pool; the classification engine (correctly, per the no-silent-fallback rule) refuses a crypto pair without DBS; and a catch-and-continue swallowed the refusal — so the pattern pool's own signal loop had generated nothing since April, 592 silent failures on July 17 alone, zero after the fix deployed. Candidate flow restored; every admission gate (Net Expectancy, exploration budget, slots, sizing) unchanged; the VTS lanes verified clean; the swallowed-hard-contract audit rides the end-of-phase runtime audit (#522). Kyle's same-morning RTB fixes shipped in the push: header/data alignment, the degenerate S.Wgt column removed (its kill-or-keep investigation scheduled as its own batch immediately before the runtime audit, per Kyle), and a queue-Duration column added.

**P19-B8.5a shipped 2026-07-13 — the pre-switch-on cleanup: the retired score is out of every trading decision, and the money-math check now guards the front door** (Claude New; Kyle green-light same day; Langston all four reviews PASS + the other Claude's independent second pass - P19_B8_5a_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). Four structural fixes landed together so paper trading starts with clean, trustworthy data: the picker's decisive number no longer carries the old finalScore (replaced by the measured win-rate from 12,000+ historical trades, set per asset class in the database — changeable without touching code); the net-expectancy check moved from the last moment before a trade opens to the FRONT — the SQE now refuses any signal whose expected value after fees is not positive, so the ready-to-buy queue only ever holds genuinely tradeable signals, while the old open-moment check stays as a hard safety backstop; the broken confidence number can no longer scale position sizes (it was quietly driving up to 4x bigger bets on empirically worse trades); and every VTS trade now records the honest inputs needed for the coming recalibration. The retired finalScore gate is still WATCHED — each signal logs what the old gate would have said, building the evidence for its formal burial from real paper results. Honest riders: the proof that the new gates fire correctly on live traffic is a named switch-on-day obligation (the pipeline is dormant until then), and the verification surfaced a real ops find — the server's console log has been silently dead since April — which now has a named fix landing before we depend on it. One process lesson recorded: a push briefly swept a teammate's in-progress line from a shared bookkeeping file; the automated checks caught it, fixed in minutes.

**P19-B8.5f shipped 2026-07-22 — trades finally have a time limit, and the place that kept losing it can no longer lose anything else silently** (Claude New; combines #549 + #550, which turned out to be one defect with three faces — B8.5g was retired into it rather than split artificially). **What was wrong:** the system stamps a maximum holding time on every signal, and a single place that rebuilds a trade's details from a hand-written list quietly dropped it. The exit check only runs when that value is present, so it never ran — **no trade has ever closed on time, in the entire history of closed trades, and none of the fifteen open positions carried a limit.** The same drop also explains why other columns were blank on some trades and populated on others: two different routes through one place, which had been misread as patchy data. **What changed:** the value is carried through, and — more durably — the type it travels in now *requires* it, so a future omission fails the build instead of going unnoticed for weeks. The narrower fix would have been to add the one field back; that was rejected because a hand-maintained list of fields to check is the same kind of object that failed here. **What was deliberately NOT done:** the same drop starves a volatility figure that the trailing exit depends on. Restoring it would switch trailing exits on mid-soak, because that behaviour is gated on the value itself — so it is Kyle's decision (#556), surfaced and not shipped. **Caught before it shipped:** turning the time limit on would have started labelling those closes 'UNKNOWN' in the trade tables, because that label was never finished — nobody bothered, since the feature had never once run. Fixed in the same batch, and the general lesson recorded: **a risk rated low *because some code never runs* becomes real the moment you make that code run.** Langston reviewed and approved at every gate, re-deriving from the code rather than the write-up. **Held open on purpose** until a trade opened after the deploy proves the carry in production — the unit tests prove the drop is fixed in the bench, not that the live path persists it.

**B-STAGING-LIVENESS-WATCH shipped 2026-07-16 — staging can no longer go dark silently, and the trading engine survives restarts on its own** (Claude New; two days ahead of the Friday deadline; Langston Step-1/2/4 ALL PASS - B_STAGING_LIVENESS_WATCH_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). Two problems died together. First, the July-15 outage class: a watchdog now runs OUTSIDE the app every five minutes checking that the site answers, the process is up, and the engine is running when it should be — and it can raise an alert even when the app is too broken to raise its own (its emergency alert format is pinned by a build-breaking test so it can never silently drift), it reports its own death, and it proves its liveness weekly. A second small probe on the other server pings the staging machine from outside and pushes a phone notification if the whole box goes dark — the one failure the on-box watchdog structurally cannot see. Both alarm paths were proven by controlled drills the same evening. Second, the restart halt (#520): an old startup cleanup routine — written before auto-resume existed — was marking every running trading session as abandoned before the resume logic could find it, which is why the engine silently stopped on every deploy all day and had to be manually restarted. The routine is deleted outright, resume now owns the decision alone, and the fix proved itself twice the same evening: both subsequent deploy restarts resumed the engine unattended, same session, all positions intact. **Same evening, Kyle's override on the net-expectancy re-check (#523):** the open-moment blocking re-check — which he correctly called a backdoor gate around the prior decision that the RTB refresh suffices — was converted to a watch-and-alarm in paper mode after code-proof that the refresh itself evicts negative-expectancy signals; live mode keeps the block pending Kyle's ratification at the end-of-phase audit (#522), per Langston's real-money risk flag.

**P19-B8.8 shipped 2026-07-16 — the position sizer can no longer trade on made-up risk numbers** (Claude New; Kyle's fix-on-find directive pulled it ahead of the queue; Langston Step-1/2/4 ALL PASS - P19_B8_8_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). The trace behind #516 found the most serious hidden fallback of the whole cleanup arc: the position sizer itself carried backup numbers it would silently substitute if it ever failed to read Kyle's risk settings — including one that would quietly raise the portfolio exposure cap to 100% with zero log evidence. It was proven three ways that a missing setting can only ever mean a real fault (the database forbids empty values, the sizer receives a whole settings row or nothing, and both live rows were queried and confirmed populated), so the whole family was retired in one design: on any unreadable input the sizer now refuses that one signal loudly and keeps running — never a fabricated number — and if refusals keep happening ten in a row, a system alert fires. The same sweep caught siblings nobody had scoped: a defaulted kill-switch threshold, two safety checks that could pass on a garbage number (now fail-closed), a goal-feasibility check that assumed 100% exposure on a failed read, a config page that served an entirely fictional settings row when the real one was missing (now an honest error), and a private settings copy inside the validation harness. Step-8 also surfaced that the trading engine silently stops on every server restart and has been manually resumed after each deploy — that repair is folded into Friday's staging-watchdog batch (#520). The first real xStock close is still pending (the two candidate rows turned out to be maker orders that expired unfilled — correct lifecycle, not closes).

**P19-B8.7 shipped 2026-07-16 — the trading tables now tell the truth Kyle asked them to** (Claude New; Kyle's morning screenshot findings + the VTS-as-reference mirror directive; Langston all four reviews PASS - P19_B8_7_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). Kyle's three findings each turned out to be real and each had a different cause: the class column was blank because the server never sent that field; the strategy column was blank because the text was rendering white-on-white — present all along but invisible whenever a strategy wasn't in a stale hand-kept color list; and the slot count "of 5" came from a display-only calculation the trading engine never actually enforces, which was also producing a false OVER LIMIT warning. All three fixed at the source, and the paper tables gained thirteen of the VTS reference columns (side, TEC state, signal/pattern, stop price, decision-time edge and rank, entry time, duration, and the target/stop pair on closed trades). The dig also unearthed a family of hidden built-in defaults that would silently substitute made-up limits if a settings read ever failed — including one that would have quietly set the trade-concurrency cap; those now halt admissions loudly for a beat instead, with the engine loop surviving. Three honest deferrals got named homes rather than quiet burial: the remaining sizing-side defaults (their own next batch — a sizer can't just skip a beat, so the fix needs design), four columns whose data the active path doesn't capture yet, and the discovery that live mode's trade tables were never wired at all (scheduled with the Phase-21 live-activation work). Verified by navigating the staging tables and reading real rows, per Kyle's new standing rule. | Kyle rulings pending: delete the redundant analytics box; which Paper columns to add into VTS.

**P19-B8.6 shipped 2026-07-15 — resting maker exits are live in paper mode, so the fee-saving question is now measured on BOTH sides of every trade** (Claude New; Kyle's urgency directive that observing entry-only “doesn't make any sense”; Langston Step-1/2 PASS, Step-4 two rounds to CHANGES-cleared, Step-8 PASS - P19_B8_6_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). When an open paper trade touches its profit target, the system now rests a sell order AT the target for up to an hour instead of paying the higher taker fee immediately: if the market comes to it, the exit fills at the cheaper maker fee with zero slippage by construction; if the deadline passes, it converts to the normal exit and honestly books the taker cost; a stop always wins instantly, resting order or not. Every outcome is stamped with the exact rested price, how long it waited, and which fee applied — the evidence needed to judge whether maker exits genuinely save money (and what a favorable gap through the target forfeits). Langston's review caught two things that mattered: expired resting exits would have lost their price record through a subtle database re-read (fixed so the record travels explicitly on every path), and part of the previous day's pricing overhaul had never actually been committed despite being verified — it shipped with this batch and was re-proven live. Riding along: the Open Trades page crash (a display label asking the server for a number it never sends) was root-caused and fixed, and the position-size dial got its measured correction to land trades at ~$150. The first real resting-exit event is an open live-watch; the VTS mirror of this build waits at #513 until the paper fill model is validated.

**P19-B8.5c shipped 2026-07-14 — trading costs are now counted in real dollars everywhere, and the broken calculator is gone, not patched** (Claude New; Kyle's opens-drop investigation ask; Langston all four reviews PASS, second pass re-derived at the live machine - P19_B8_5c_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). The investigation into why virtual trades dropped off found an old units slip: two record-keeping calculators were counting round-trip trading costs as pennies instead of dollars (a $900 stock booked ~2 cents of cost where ~$17.50 was owed, with the error reversing direction below $1 — both predicted effects were observed in the data, clinching it). The admission gates had already been silently corrected by earlier work — so the 'drop' was the honest fee wall arriving, not a malfunction — but the broken calculators kept writing fictional numbers into the learning records daily. Rather than repairing them, the review process improved the fix: Langston asked why two calculators should exist at all, and the answer was they shouldn't — both were deleted and everything now reads from the single correct source, making the error class structurally impossible rather than merely fixed. The clinching proof came at his second pass: counting the impossible-number signature across the whole day and splitting at the exact second the new code took over — 938 violations before, zero after. The record books now carry clear date boundaries so future calibration never mixes the eras; combined with the previous day's DI fix, all virtual-trade learning data from July 14 forward is clean on both fronts. **P19-B8.5d ✅ DONE 2026-07-16** (sizing tune-3: Kyle spec 100%/6.67%/$2,250 → ~$145 fills proven live + §9.3 UI-walked; config-only; Langston Step-1/rev-2/ref-discharge ALL PASS). **P19-B8.9a ✅ DONE 2026-07-16** (source-tag honesty: 4 mislabels + the stale-last-resort venue-tag hole closed; venue predicate + honest updateCache; 28/28 tests, deployed 21:05Z, §9.3 walk PASS; corrected REST baseline 0.28/sec peak; Langston Step-4 APPROVED; sub-batch of the in-flight P19-B8.9).

**B-EVIDENCE-SINK deployed 2026-07-14 — the switch-on's proof-of-behavior now lands in a permanent, queryable store instead of a log that erases itself** (Claude Old; Kyle "complete the evidence home" directive; Langston Step-1/2/4/8 all PASS — B_EVIDENCE_SINK_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). The third of four pieces that must be ready before paper trading turns back on. When the flip happens, the system will emit three proof-signals we need to watch — whether the retired score would have blocked each trade, how often the last-moment money check rejects (which should be near-zero if the front-door check is working), and which entry style it picks — but those were only being printed to a running log that now auto-erases every couple of days, far short of the weeks-long validation window. This builds a dedicated database table for them, covered by our tiered never-drop storage, written through a fail-safe path that degrades to the old log and can never slow or crash a live trade (proven by a test that deliberately breaks the writer). It also captures, for each entry-style decision, the exact cost assumptions used — the raw material the later calibration needs — so we record it once here instead of building a second store later. The sink is live and verified on staging with zero rows (correct — the real proof only flows once trading is on); proving the signals actually populate is a named switch-on-day task. Two review catches worth noting: a teammate found a gap where a rare missing value could silently drop evidence, fixed uniformly; and the verification step caught that registering the table's retention rule wasn't enough — the background jobs that enforce tiering use fixed lists my table wasn't on, so it would have piled up forever and run out of partitions in a year — fixed before close. That's the verification discipline earning its keep again. One pre-flip piece (the backtest baseline) is done; this one is in final review; the flip still waits for Kyle's explicit go.

**P19-B8.5b shipped 2026-07-13 — the record-keeping is complete for honest replay, and the stand-in DI is gone from both sides** (Claude New; Kyle's capture directive; Langston all four reviews PASS, second pass independently re-derived at the machine - P19_B8_5b_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). Two long-standing honesty gaps closed before the switch-on. First, every trade evaluation now saves the six numbers the strategies were actually looking at when they decided, plus a fingerprint of the exact price bars in view — so a future replay can PROVE it saw identical data instead of reconstructing and hoping (the earlier replay study topped out at about 71% agreement precisely because these were missing; a scheduled three-week re-check will measure the improvement on real accrued data). Second, the stand-in value that had been passed to the profitability math in place of the real DI since February — a leftover patch that made every VTS trade's win-probability estimate partly fictional — is deleted on both the crypto and xStock sides, with the real DI carried through instead; where no real DI exists the record now honestly says "none" rather than inventing a number. One genuine defect was caught by our own verification step: the new fingerprint silently failed to save on the live server even though every pre-release test passed — the test environment tolerated a coding pattern the production server doesn't, and the code's own safety net swallowed the error. The lesson is recorded permanently: passing tests is not proof the capture works; counting real saved rows on the live server is, and that check is now a standing part of every capture batch. A discovered wrinkle got its own tracked home rather than a quiet patch: the crypto side and the xStock side compute their DI differently under the same name, and unifying them is scheduled for the calibration phase where the evidence lives. Two of the four pre-flip gates are now satisfied; the remaining two (the dead server log and the backtest baseline) are Claude Old's lanes.

**P19-B8.5e shipped 2026-07-22 — each xStock now gets its own price-freshness limit instead of one shared 90-second rule ✅** (Claude New; `#548` RESOLVED). Every xStock used to share a single rule: refuse to act on a price older than 90 seconds. That one number was wrong in both directions at once — long enough for the fastest-moving xStock we hold to run past its stop unnoticed, yet strict enough that the calmest one was refused management 49 times in a single day of ordinary quiet trading. Symbols whose risk-per-second differs about elevenfold cannot share a constant. The limit is now worked out per xStock and per trade, from how fast that particular xStock actually moves and how much room the trade still has before its stop — so **a trade sitting close to its stop gets the strictest limit**, which is right, because that is exactly when acting on a wrong price costs the most. The old shared setting was retired outright rather than left dormant. Verified on staging: all fifteen open trades show live current prices beside their entry prices with distances to target and stop filled in — those were the ones going blank. **Review caught two genuine holes in the new work, both fixed before close:** a speed measurement taken while an xStock was quiet would have made the system *more* willing to trust an old price once that xStock got busy (found by Claude Analyst — the exact opposite of the intent, and it co-occurs with stops being hit), and the fallback speed estimate never expired (found by Langston). **An incident is recorded rather than smoothed over:** the deploy took staging down for about two minutes because a new startup safety check was added without registering the settings it reads, and the check's own error message blamed the wrong cause — the standing lesson written into the code is that *a fail-closed check must not assert a cause it has not verified*. **What this does NOT fix, stated plainly so it is not mistaken for solved:** near a stop with a stale price the trade is exposed whichever way the system chooses (`#563`, the structural fix — an exit order resting at the exchange rather than one that depends on our own process staying alive); the speed measure still lags a sudden volatility spike (`#566`); and **nothing checks whether a price is believable — only whether it is recent** (`#567`).


**B-GOV-INTEGRITY-1 shipped 2026-07-10 — closing an alert now leaves an honest record of who, when, and why** (Claude Old; Kyle governance-integrity directive; Langston-reviewed - B_GOV_INTEGRITY_1_COMPLETION_REPORT.md). Kyle core worry was that problems get "acknowledged, falsely marked verified, and pushed aside as done." The alert system made that impossible to even detect: it recorded who claimed an alert but wrote nothing at all when one was closed — 249 of 249 past closures had no record of who closed them, when, or on what basis. Now every close must carry four things: when, who says they closed it (a claim, labeled as one), which channel it actually came through (stamped by the system, not the person), and a checkable reason — a real reference or an honest "no evidence given," with lazy words like "looks fine" refused. The 250 old closures were filled in truthfully: what was genuinely known was reconstructed, what was never recorded was marked unknown, nothing invented — all 250 checked afterwards, zero with a fabricated date or identity. Two side-fixes rode along: low-priority governance warnings now actually reach the chat instead of being silently dropped (117 were), and a hole that let mislabeled alert categories pile up (13 names against a list of 6) is closed. It interlocks with Claude New checker repair — the checker now stamps each of its ~140 closures-per-cycle with a re-checkable reference, and a guard test locks that handoff. One process slip surfaced and got its own fix: because the two AI sessions share one working copy, an unreviewed commit briefly rode up on the other publish (no harm — passed every check, nothing was live), now guarded by a read-every-commit rule and a proposed permanent isolation.

**B-STORAGE-HARDEN Wave D landed 2026-07-08 — the biggest single storage consumer (xStock price-quote history) now files data day-by-day and captures it less often, closing the batch ✅** (Claude Old; Kyle-authorized autonomous; Langston-reviewed; crew-consensus on the capture rate · `B_STORAGE_HARDEN_WAVE_D_COMPLETION_REPORT.md`). Two efficiency levers on the #1 disk hog. First (the structural win): that data used to be filed one month at a time, so we could only free space a whole month at a time (up to ~60 days held). It now files one day at a time, so we hold a true rolling 30 days and free space daily — switched over cleanly at the Aug 1 month boundary without ever touching or rewriting the existing 60 GB of live data, and proven end-to-end by aging a test day through the whole keep-forever pipeline. Second (the capture-rate lever): I measured the live per-symbol timing during market hours and, with Langston and Claude New, landed by consensus on a conservative setting that roughly triples the storage saving on captures while keeping the liquid names (Apple, Nvidia, the big ETFs) comfortably inside our price-freshness limit — only about ten names are affected (and a Kyle-requested dig found these aren't thin tokens at all — they're mid/large names like Danaher and Cardinal Health that tick fast on average but occasionally go quiet for a few seconds; a new thin listing would instead be slow at any capture rate, a separate pre-existing limit). Kyle CONFIRMED the conservative setting, on the condition we add a weekly report that tracks any real trading opportunities we lose — built as an immediate follow-up. Along the way I caught and fixed a real hidden bug (a scheduled "make future storage slots" job that had been silently failing every run on a missing database credential) and surfaced a separate, unrelated data-feed problem (the xStock one-minute price bars intermittently stall) that's now tracked with its own follow-up. Confirmed the capture change does NOT touch the one-minute decision bars the strategies actually trade off. This is the last wave — **B-STORAGE-HARDEN (all three waves) CLOSED 2026-07-08.** Follow-up: a weekly xStock freshness / opportunity-loss monitor (B-XSTOCK-FRESHNESS-MONITOR), Kyle's condition for the conservative capture rate, built immediately after.

**B-CREW-COORD PARKED 2026-07-22 — recorded as parked, because "parked" was living only in one session's private notes** (Claude Analyst; #554; BATCH_CATALOG carries the detail). The batch was building a shared board so the three Claude sessions and Langston can see who is holding which files before editing them. Kyle redirected me to higher-priority work partway through, which was the right call — but I never wrote down anywhere visible that it had stopped. A scheduled check noticed code had been pushed with no matching paperwork and raised it. **The alert was correct and is the reason this entry exists.** **The thing the alert shook loose, which nobody had noticed:** the batch had already pushed a database change, and that change is registered in the list the deploy process runs. **So the next deployment of anything, by anyone, will quietly create a table belonging to a batch nobody is working on.** Judged harmless — nothing in the trading path reads that table, which was a deliberate design fence, so it cannot affect how trades are chosen or placed — but deliberately not left silent. It stays registered (removing it is churn, and creating it early makes resuming safer) and is now written down so whoever deploys next is not surprised by a table appearing. **The general lesson worth more than this instance: parking a batch is not a no-op. "I stopped working on it" is not the same as "nothing of it is running," and a half-finished batch can leave something live in the deploy path.**

**B-COMMS-CHUNK-FIX shipped 2026-07-22 — long messages to Langston stopped being silently cut in half, and code references can no longer be torn apart mid-word** (Claude Analyst; Kyle-assigned; Langston APPROVED as-is after verifying the live sources — B_COMMS_CHUNK_FIX_COMPLETION_REPORT.md, #553). Anything over 2000 characters was split into separate messages and every piece after the first was discarded before Langston ever saw it — no error on either side. A second, worse defect was found in the pre-audit: a phone-notify-flagged dispatch to him was dropped ENTIRELY, not truncated. Both fixed. Both of Langston's review findings were fixed in-batch rather than deferred, including one CC-A and CC-B independently escalated: a mid-word cut through a `file:line` reference does not fail loudly, it yields a plausible-but-wrong coordinate, so a reader checks the wrong line and concludes a TRUE claim is FALSE. Cuts now land only on whitespace. Adoption cost is zero — verified in production when CC-B's and CC-A's own dispatches reassembled with no change on their part. Recorded as debt: the deploy preceded Step-4 review, an exception earned by change-class + active-corruption + ~10s reversibility, NOT a precedent.
